**Examples:**
1. The company reported a substantial increase in quarterly earnings.
2. There is substantial interest in the new product line.

---

### 364. inform → notify

**Meaning:** to formally tell someone about something

**Examples:**
1. Please notify the vendor of the revised delivery schedule.
2. We will notify all employees of the policy update by email.

---

### 365. grow → expand

**Meaning:** to increase in size, scope, or reach

**Examples:**
1. The startup plans to expand into three new regions next year.
2. We need to expand our customer base before the next funding round.

---

### 366. interested → invested

**Meaning:** emotionally or financially committed to an outcome

**Examples:**
1. Leadership is deeply invested in the success of this initiative.
2. Employees who feel invested in the mission tend to stay longer.

---

### 367. instead → alternatively

**Meaning:** as another option or choice in place of something

**Examples:**
1. Alternatively, we could postpone the launch until Q3.
2. The client asked us to consider a subscription model alternatively.

---

### 368. guess → estimate

**Meaning:** an approximate calculation or judgment

**Examples:**
1. Our analysts estimate a fifteen percent rise in demand.
2. Please estimate the project cost before we submit the proposal.

---

### 369. great → outstanding

**Meaning:** exceptionally good or remarkable

**Examples:**
1. The team delivered an outstanding performance this quarter.
2. Client feedback on the new service has been outstanding.

---

### 370. indicate → denote

**Meaning:** to point to or signify something

**Examples:**
1. A red flag in the report denotes an unresolved compliance issue.
2. Rising churn rates denote a need for better customer support.

---

### 371. kind → considerate

**Meaning:** thoughtful and mindful of others' needs

**Examples:**
1. The manager was considerate in scheduling around the team's workload.
2. A considerate approach to layoffs helped preserve company morale.

---

### 372. keen → enthusiastic

**Meaning:** showing eager interest or excitement

**Examples:**
1. The new hire was enthusiastic about joining the innovation team.
2. Investors seemed enthusiastic about the company's growth plans.

---

### 373. invite → solicit

**Meaning:** to formally ask for something, such as feedback or bids

**Examples:**
1. We will solicit proposals from three vendors before deciding.
2. The survey aims to solicit honest feedback from employees.

---

### 374. gap → discrepancy

**Meaning:** a noticeable difference between two things that should match

**Examples:**
1. The audit revealed a discrepancy between reported and actual sales.
2. There is a discrepancy between the forecast and current performance.

---

### 375. hope → anticipate

**Meaning:** to expect something to happen, often with confidence

**Examples:**
1. We anticipate strong demand during the holiday season.
2. The finance team anticipates a rebound in revenue next quarter.

---

### 376. honest → candid

**Meaning:** open and truthful, even when the truth is uncomfortable

**Examples:**
1. The manager gave candid feedback during the performance review.
2. We appreciate the client's candid assessment of our proposal.

---

### 377. hurt → damage

**Meaning:** to cause harm to something, such as a reputation or finances

**Examples:**
1. The scandal could damage the brand's reputation for years.
2. Delayed deliveries damaged our relationship with key retailers.

---

### 378. hurry → expedite

**Meaning:** to speed up a process or action

**Examples:**
1. We asked the supplier to expedite the shipment.
2. The team worked overtime to expedite the product launch.

---

### 379. interesting → compelling

**Meaning:** strongly engaging or persuasive

**Examples:**
1. The startup presented a compelling case for investment.
2. Our data tells a compelling story about customer loyalty.

---

### 380. invest → allocate

**Meaning:** to assign resources for a specific purpose

**Examples:**
1. The board decided to allocate additional funds to research.
2. We will allocate more staff to handle the holiday rush.

---

### 381. judge → evaluate

**Meaning:** to assess the quality or value of something

**Examples:**
1. The committee will evaluate all vendor proposals next week.
2. Managers evaluate performance quarterly using set criteria.

---

### 382. jump → surge

**Meaning:** a sudden and significant increase

**Examples:**
1. Online sales saw a surge after the marketing campaign.
2. The company experienced a surge in applications this month.

---

### 383. healthy → robust

**Meaning:** strong, vigorous, and functioning well

**Examples:**
1. The company reported robust earnings despite market volatility.
2. Our supply chain remained robust throughout the disruption.

---

### 384. hint → indication

**Meaning:** a sign that suggests something without stating it directly

**Examples:**
1. Early sales figures gave a clear indication of demand.
2. There was no indication the client planned to cancel the contract.

---

### 385. hide → conceal

**Meaning:** to keep something from being seen or known

**Examples:**
1. The report should not conceal any material risks to investors.

---

### 386. isolate → segregate

**Meaning:** to separate something from the rest

**Examples:**
1. We segregated the faulty units before shipping the batch.
2. The audit team segregated duties to prevent fraud.

---

### 387. glad → pleased

**Meaning:** feeling satisfaction or happiness about something

**Examples:**
1. We are pleased to announce record quarterly earnings.
2. The client was pleased with the revised timeline.

---

### 388. grateful → appreciative

**Meaning:** feeling or showing thankfulness

**Examples:**
1. The team is appreciative of leadership's support during the transition.
2. We remain appreciative of our clients' continued loyalty.

---

### 389. guarantee → ensure

**Meaning:** to make certain that something will happen

**Examples:**
1. Quality checks ensure the product meets industry standards.
2. We must ensure compliance before the audit begins.

---

### 390. harsh → stringent

**Meaning:** strict and demanding, often referring to rules or conditions

**Examples:**
1. The new regulations impose stringent reporting requirements.
2. Suppliers must meet stringent quality controls.

---

### 391. harmful → detrimental

**Meaning:** causing damage or negative effects

**Examples:**
1. Frequent turnover can be detrimental to team morale.
2. Ignoring customer complaints could be detrimental to the brand.

---

### 392. hardworking → diligent

**Meaning:** showing careful and persistent effort

**Examples:**
1. The diligent efforts of the support team reduced ticket backlogs.
2. Her diligent approach to research impressed the entire committee.

---

### 393. helpful → constructive

**Meaning:** useful and intended to improve something

**Examples:**
1. The client offered constructive feedback on the prototype.
2. Managers are encouraged to give constructive criticism regularly.

---

### 394. hopeful → optimistic

**Meaning:** confident and positive about a future outcome

**Examples:**
1. Executives are optimistic about next quarter's earnings.
2. The sales team remains optimistic despite slower demand.

---

### 395. hasty → premature

**Meaning:** done too quickly, before it is appropriate

**Examples:**
1. A premature product launch could damage customer trust.
2. The board rejected the premature decision to cut the budget.

---

### 396. humble → unassuming

**Meaning:** modest and not seeking attention

**Examples:**
1. Despite her success, the CEO remains unassuming in meetings.
2. The founder's unassuming style earned respect across the team.

---

### 397. hazard → liability

**Meaning:** a source of risk or potential loss

**Examples:**
1. Unsafe equipment poses a liability to the company.
2. Poor data security practices are a major liability for the firm.

---

### 398. hitch → complication

**Meaning:** an unexpected problem that disrupts progress

**Examples:**
1. A complication with customs delayed the shipment.
2. We encountered a complication during the software migration.

---

### 399. insist → assert

**Meaning:** to state something firmly and confidently

**Examples:**
1. The vendor continued to assert that the delay was unavoidable.
2. Management asserted that the layoffs were necessary for survival.

---

### 400. interrupt → disrupt

**Meaning:** to interfere with the normal flow of something

**Examples:**
1. The outage disrupted operations for nearly six hours.
2. Supply chain issues continue to disrupt manufacturing schedules.

---

### 401. intend → endeavor

**Meaning:** to make a determined effort to achieve something

**Examples:**
1. We endeavor to deliver every project on time and on budget.
2. The company endeavors to reduce its carbon footprint by 2030.

---

### 402. ignore → disregard

**Meaning:** to intentionally pay no attention to something

**Examples:**
1. The firm cannot disregard the auditor's recommendations.
2. Employees should not disregard the updated safety protocols.

---

### 403. imagine → envision

**Meaning:** to form a mental picture of a future possibility

**Examples:**
1. The founders envision a fully automated supply chain within five years.
2. Leadership envisions expanding into three new markets by 2028.

---

### 404. instant → instantaneous

**Meaning:** happening immediately, without any delay

**Examples:**
1. The new system provides instantaneous transaction confirmations.
2. Customers now receive instantaneous updates on order status.

---

### 405. immediate → expeditious

**Meaning:** acting or done with speed and efficiency

**Examples:**
1. We appreciate your expeditious handling of the refund request.
2. The legal team took an expeditious approach to the contract review.

---

### 406. inspire → galvanize

**Meaning:** to motivate people into action, often with excitement

**Examples:**
1. The CEO's speech helped galvanize the team ahead of the launch.
2. The campaign galvanized employees to embrace the new strategy.

---

### 407. itemize → enumerate

**Meaning:** to list things individually and in detail

2. The contract enumerates each party's specific obligations.

---

### 408. junk → scrap

**Meaning:** to discard or get rid of something no longer useful

**Examples:**
1. The committee voted to scrap the outdated marketing plan.
2. We had to scrap the initial prototype after failed testing.

---

### 409. jam → congestion

**Meaning:** a state of being overcrowded or blocked, slowing progress

**Examples:**
1. Server congestion caused delays during the flash sale.
2. Warehouse congestion slowed down order fulfillment last week.

---

### 410. jolt → disruption

**Meaning:** a sudden shock that upsets normal operations

1. The supplier's bankruptcy caused a major disruption to production.
2. Currency fluctuations gave the export business a serious disruption.

---

### 411. jumble → disarray

**Meaning:** a state of confusion or disorder

---

### 412. jot → record

**Meaning:** to write down information for later reference

**Examples:**
1. Please record the key decisions from today's meeting.
2. The assistant recorded all action items during the call.

---

### 413. juggle → coordinate

**Examples:**

---

### 414. joint → collaborative

**Meaning:** involving shared effort between two or more parties

**Examples:**
1. The two firms launched a collaborative research initiative.
2. This is a collaborative effort between marketing and product teams.

---

### 415. journey → trajectory

**Meaning:** the path or direction something follows over time

**Examples:**
1. The company's growth trajectory has impressed investors.
2. We mapped the customer's trajectory from first click to purchase.

---

### 416. joy → gratification

**Meaning:** a feeling of pleasure and satisfaction

**Examples:**
1. There is great gratification in seeing a project through to completion.
2. Employee surveys show high gratification with the new benefits package.

---

### 417. jaded → disillusioned

**Meaning:** having lost enthusiasm or trust due to repeated disappointment

**Examples:**

2. Investors grew disillusioned following several missed forecasts.

---

### 418. just → equitable

**Meaning:** fair and impartial in treatment or distribution

**Examples:**
1. The company adopted a more equitable bonus structure this year.
2. HR aims to ensure an equitable hiring process across departments.

---

### 419. gross → aggregate

**Meaning:** a total amount combined from all individual parts

**Examples:**
1. The aggregate revenue across all divisions rose by ten percent.
2. Please report the aggregate figures before the board meeting.

---

### 420. gradual → incremental

**Meaning:** happening in small, steady steps over time

**Examples:**
1. The company is taking an incremental approach to digital transformation.
2. We expect incremental improvements in efficiency each quarter.

---

### 421. genuine → authentic

**Meaning:** real and true, not fake or forced

**Examples:**
1. Customers respond well to authentic brand storytelling.
2. The CEO's authentic apology helped restore public trust.

---

### 422. generous → magnanimous

**Meaning:** exceptionally kind and giving, especially toward competitors or rivals

**Examples:**
1. The founder was magnanimous in crediting the whole team for the win.
2. The acquiring company made a magnanimous offer to retain staff.

---

### 423. gentle → tactful

**Meaning:** careful and considerate in how something is communicated

**Examples:**
1. The manager gave tactful feedback on the failed pitch.
2. A tactful response can defuse a tense client conversation.

---

### 424. govern → regulate

**Meaning:** to control or oversee something through rules

**Examples:**
1. New policies regulate how customer data is stored and shared.
2. The compliance team regulates vendor conduct across all contracts.

---

### 425. gossip → hearsay

**Meaning:** unverified information passed informally between people

**Examples:**
1. Decisions should never be based on hearsay from the rumor mill.
2. HR discourages spreading hearsay about ongoing investigations.

---

### 426. glitch → malfunction

**Meaning:** a temporary fault or error in a system

**Examples:**
1. A software malfunction delayed the payment processing system.
2. The outage was traced to a malfunction in the server cluster.

---

### 427. gimmick → ploy

**Meaning:** a scheme used to attract attention, often seen as insincere

**Examples:**
1. Critics called the discount a marketing ploy rather than real value.
2. The campaign avoided any ploy that might undermine brand trust.

---

### 428. grumble → lament

**Meaning:** to express dissatisfaction or regret about something

**Examples:**
1. Employees continued to lament the lack of remote work options.
2. Investors lamented the delayed rollout of the new platform.

---

### 429. guest → attendee

**Meaning:** a person present at an event or meeting

**Examples:**
1. Over two hundred attendees registered for the annual conference.
2. Each attendee will receive a copy of the keynote presentation.

---

### 430. grip → mastery

**Meaning:** a strong command or control over a subject or skill

**Examples:**
1. The new analyst quickly gained mastery of the reporting tools.
2. Years of experience gave her mastery over complex negotiations.

---

### 431. ground → foundation

**Meaning:** the basic underlying support or reasoning for something

**Examples:**
1. Solid market research forms the foundation of our strategy.
2. Trust is the foundation of any successful partnership.

---

### 432. grab → secure

**Meaning:** to obtain and hold onto something, often quickly

**Examples:**
1. The firm moved fast to secure the contract before competitors bid.
2. We managed to secure additional funding ahead of the deadline.

---

### 433. gauge → assess

**Meaning:** to measure or evaluate the level of something

**Examples:**
1. We conducted a survey to assess customer satisfaction.
2. Management wants to assess the risk before approving the deal.

---

### 434. glimpse → preview

**Meaning:** a brief early look at something before its full release

**Examples:**
1. Investors received a preview of the upcoming product line.
2. The demo gave clients a preview of the redesigned dashboard.

---

### 435. gist → essence

**Meaning:** the central meaning or most important point of something

**Examples:**
1. The executive summary captures the essence of the full report.
2. Her feedback got to the essence of what customers really wanted.

---

### 436. greet → acknowledge

**Meaning:** to formally recognize or respond to someone or something

**Examples:**
1. Please acknowledge receipt of the signed contract by email.
2. The CEO acknowledged the team's efforts during the town hall.

---

### 437. habit → practice

**Meaning:** a customary way of behaving or doing something

**Examples:**
1. Regular check-ins have become standard practice on this team.
2. Documenting decisions is good practice for future reference.

---

### 438. hunch → intuition

**Meaning:** an instinctive feeling about something, without full evidence

**Examples:**
1. The founder's intuition about the market proved correct.
2. Data confirmed what her intuition had suggested all along.

---

### 439. hesitant → apprehensive

**Meaning:** uneasy or anxious about a decision or outcome

**Examples:**
1. Some investors remained apprehensive about entering the new market.
2. Employees were apprehensive about the upcoming restructuring.

---

### 440. hiccup → setback

**Meaning:** a minor problem that temporarily hinders progress

**Examples:**
1. A minor setback in production pushed the launch back a week.
2. Despite a few setbacks, the rollout stayed largely on schedule.

---

### 441. hunt → pursue

**Meaning:** to actively search for or work toward something

**Examples:**
1. The firm continues to pursue partnerships in emerging markets.
2. Our recruiters are pursuing candidates with specialized skills.

---

### 442. hush → quell

**Meaning:** to suppress or calm something, such as concern or unrest

**Examples:**
1. The statement helped quell rumors of an impending layoff.
2. Management moved quickly to quell employee concerns after the announcement.

---

### 443. hype → publicize

**Meaning:** to promote something widely to attract attention

**Examples:**
1. The team plans to publicize the launch through social media.
2. We will publicize the new partnership at next week's summit.

---

### 444. horrible → dire

**Meaning:** extremely serious or urgent, often referring to a bad situation

**Examples:**
1. The audit revealed dire gaps in the company's financial controls.
2. Analysts warned of dire consequences if costs kept rising.

---

### 445. hollow → vacuous

**Meaning:** lacking real substance or meaning

**Examples:**
1. Investors dismissed the vacuous promises made in the pitch deck.
2. The apology felt vacuous without any concrete follow-up actions.

---

### 446. heavy → onerous

**Meaning:** burdensome and difficult to bear

**Examples:**
1. The new compliance rules impose an onerous reporting burden.
2. Employees complained about the onerous overtime requirements.

---

### 447. instinct → discernment

**Meaning:** the ability to judge or perceive things clearly and wisely

**Examples:**
1. Her discernment helped the team avoid a risky investment.
2. Strong discernment is essential when evaluating new vendors.

---

### 448. initiate → spearhead

**Meaning:** to lead and drive forward a new project or effort

**Examples:**
1. She will spearhead the company's sustainability initiative.
2. The CTO spearheaded the migration to cloud infrastructure.

---

### 449. iffy → questionable

**Meaning:** uncertain or doubtful in reliability or validity

**Examples:**
1. The forecast relied on questionable assumptions about demand.
2. Auditors flagged several questionable entries in the ledger.

---

### 450. irritate → aggravate

**Meaning:** to make a problem or feeling worse

**Examples:**
1. Poor communication only aggravated the client's frustration.
2. Delayed responses can aggravate an already tense negotiation.

---

### 451. inquire → ascertain

**Meaning:** to find out something with certainty through investigation

**Examples:**
1. We need to ascertain the root cause before issuing a statement.
2. The auditor ascertained that the figures were accurate.

---

### 452. interfere → impede

**Meaning:** to slow down or obstruct progress

**Examples:**
1. Outdated systems continue to impede our reporting efficiency.
2. Regulatory delays impeded the rollout of the new service.

---

### 453. idle → dormant

**Meaning:** inactive or not currently in use

**Examples:**
1. The company reactivated a dormant division to meet new demand.
2. Dormant accounts were flagged during the annual audit.

---

### 454. impossible → infeasible

**Meaning:** not achievable given current resources or constraints

**Examples:**
1. The proposed timeline was deemed infeasible by the engineering team.
2. Expanding to five markets at once proved financially infeasible.

---

### 455. intense → rigorous

**Meaning:** thorough and demanding, carried out with great care

**Examples:**
1. Candidates undergo a rigorous interview process before hiring.
2. The audit involved a rigorous review of every transaction.

---

### 456. kickoff → inception

**Meaning:** the beginning or start of a project or initiative

**Examples:**
1. The budget was approved at the project's inception.
2. Stakeholder alignment is critical from the inception of any initiative.

---

### 457. knock → disparage

**Meaning:** to speak of something in a critical or belittling way

**Examples:**
1. Competitors tried to disparage our new product in the press.
2. It is unprofessional to disparage a former employer in interviews.

---

### 458. kill → terminate

**Meaning:** to formally end or discontinue something

**Examples:**
1. The board voted to terminate the underperforming project.
2. We had to terminate the vendor contract due to repeated delays.

---

### 459. know-how → expertise

**Meaning:** specialized skill or knowledge in a particular area

**Examples:**
1. The firm brings deep expertise in supply chain optimization.
2. We hired a consultant with expertise in regulatory compliance.

---

### 460. kindly → graciously

**Meaning:** in a warm, courteous, and generous manner

**Examples:**
1. The client graciously agreed to extend the payment deadline.
2. Leadership graciously acknowledged the team's extra effort.

---

### 461. knack → aptitude

**Meaning:** a natural talent or ability for something

**Examples:**
1. She has a strong aptitude for resolving client disputes.
2. The new hire showed an impressive aptitude for data analysis.

---

### 462. kink → imperfection

**Meaning:** a small flaw or problem in a system or plan

**Examples:**
1. We are working out a few imperfections in the onboarding process.
2. The pilot program revealed minor imperfections in the workflow.

---

### 463. kickstart → catalyze

**Meaning:** to trigger or accelerate the start of something

**Examples:**
1. The grant helped catalyze growth in the early-stage startup.
2. New leadership catalyzed a much-needed cultural shift.

---

### 464. kindle → ignite

**Meaning:** to spark or start a feeling or process

**Examples:**
1. The campaign was designed to ignite interest among younger buyers.
2. Her keynote ignited enthusiasm for the company's new direction.

---

### 465. keep up → sustain

**Meaning:** to maintain a level of performance or effort over time

**Examples:**
1. The team worked hard to sustain momentum after the launch.
2. Consistent quality helps sustain customer loyalty over the long term.

---

### 466. keep track → monitor

**Meaning:** to observe and check the progress of something regularly

**Examples:**
1. The dashboard lets managers monitor sales performance daily.
2. We monitor server uptime around the clock.

---

### 467. kick out → dismiss

**Meaning:** to formally remove someone from a position or group

**Examples:**
1. The board voted to dismiss the underperforming executive.
2. The vendor was dismissed from the approved supplier list.

---

### 468. kudos → commendation

**Meaning:** formal praise or recognition for an achievement

**Examples:**
1. The team received a commendation for exceeding their annual targets.
2. Leadership issued a commendation for the successful product launch.

---

### 469. keystone → cornerstone

**Meaning:** a central and essential element that everything else depends on

**Examples:**
1. Customer trust is the cornerstone of our business model.
2. This partnership will serve as the cornerstone of our expansion plan.

---

### 470. kingpin → linchpin

**Meaning:** the central person or element that holds a plan together

**Examples:**
1. The operations manager is the linchpin of our supply chain.
2. This client relationship is the linchpin of our regional strategy.

---

### 471. knee-jerk → instinctive

**Meaning:** an immediate, automatic reaction without careful thought

**Examples:**
1. The team avoided an instinctive reaction to the negative review.
2. Leadership cautioned against instinctive decisions during the crisis.

---

### 472. kosher → compliant

**Meaning:** acceptable and in line with rules or standards

**Examples:**
1. Legal confirmed the arrangement is fully compliant with regulations.
2. We need every vendor contract to be compliant before signing.

---

### 473. knit → unify

**Meaning:** to bring separate parts together into a cohesive whole

**Examples:**
1. The retreat helped unify teams across different regional offices.
2. A shared mission statement can unify employees around common goals.

---

### 474. keep out → exclude

**Meaning:** to prevent something or someone from being included

**Examples:**
1. The policy excludes competitors from accessing the beta program.
2. We had to exclude two vendors that failed the compliance check.

---

### 475. keep pace → stay competitive

**Meaning:** to remain on par with rivals or industry standards

**Examples:**
1. We must innovate constantly to stay competitive in this market.
2. The firm invested in automation to stay competitive on pricing.

---

### 476. keep calm → maintain composure

**Meaning:** to stay calm and controlled under pressure

**Examples:**
1. The negotiator managed to maintain composure during the tense talks.
2. Leaders are trained to maintain composure during a crisis.

---

### 477. keep quiet → maintain confidentiality

**Meaning:** to keep information private and undisclosed

**Examples:**
1. Employees are required to maintain confidentiality during the merger talks.
2. The consultant agreed to maintain confidentiality regarding client data.

---

### 478. keep secret → safeguard

**Meaning:** to protect something from being disclosed or lost

**Examples:**
1. The firm has strict protocols to safeguard proprietary information.
2. IT security teams work to safeguard customer data at all times.

---

### 479. keep afloat → remain solvent

**Meaning:** to continue operating without running out of funds

**Examples:**
1. The loan helped the company remain solvent during the downturn.
2. Cost-cutting measures allowed the business to remain solvent through the recession.

---

### 480. keep watch → oversee

**Meaning:** to supervise and ensure something is progressing correctly

**Examples:**
1. The project lead will oversee implementation across all regions.
2. A dedicated compliance officer oversees all vendor contracts.

---

## Section: L – O

### 481. need → require

**Meaning:** to need something in a formal or necessary sense

**Examples:**
1. The project will require additional budget approval before Q3.
2. All new hires require sign-off from HR before their start date.

---

### 482. make → generate

**Meaning:** to produce or create, often used for output like revenue or ideas

**Examples:**
1. The campaign is expected to generate significant new leads.
2. We need this report to generate actionable insights for leadership.

---

### 483. new → novel

**Meaning:** original and not seen before

**Examples:**
1. The R&D team proposed a novel approach to customer onboarding.
2. Investors were impressed by the startup's novel pricing model.

---

### 484. now → immediately

**Meaning:** without any delay

**Examples:**
1. Please escalate the outage to the vendor immediately.
2. The finance team must be notified immediately of any discrepancy.

---

### 485. more → additional

**Meaning:** extra or supplementary to what already exists

**Examples:**
1. We will need additional headcount to hit the Q4 targets.
2. The client requested additional documentation before signing.

---

### 486. many → myriad

**Meaning:** a very large, often hard-to-count number of things

**Examples:**
1. The migration involved a myriad of technical dependencies.
2. There are a myriad of factors influencing the vendor decision.

---

### 487. most → majority

**Meaning:** the greater part of a group or total

**Examples:**
1. The majority of stakeholders approved the proposed timeline.
2. A majority of customers reported satisfaction with the update.

---

### 488. look → review

**Meaning:** to examine something carefully

**Examples:**
1. Legal will review the contract before we send it to the client.
2. Please review the attached deck ahead of tomorrow's meeting.

---

### 489. next → subsequent

**Meaning:** coming after the current one in a sequence

**Examples:**
1. We will address pricing in the subsequent phase of negotiations.
2. Subsequent releases will include the requested integrations.

---

### 490. meet → convene

**Meaning:** to bring people together formally for a purpose

**Examples:**
1. The steering committee will convene on Thursday to finalize the roadmap.
2. Department heads convened to discuss the restructuring plan.

---

### 491. move → transition

**Meaning:** to shift from one state or system to another

**Examples:**
1. The team will transition to the new CRM by end of month.
2. We are transitioning our support operations to a follow-the-sun model.

---

### 492. note → document

**Meaning:** to formally record information for later reference

**Examples:**
1. Please document any changes made during the client call.
2. All exceptions must be documented in the compliance log.

---

### 493. list → enumerate

**Meaning:** to name items one by one, clearly and formally

**Examples:**
1. The proposal should enumerate all deliverables and deadlines.
2. Please enumerate the risks associated with this vendor.

---

### 494. main → primary

**Meaning:** the most important or central one

**Examples:**
1. Our primary objective this quarter is customer retention.
2. The primary concern raised by the board was cash flow.

---

### 495. must → obligated

**Meaning:** required to do something by rule or agreement

**Examples:**
1. Vendors are obligated to meet the service-level agreement.
2. Employees are obligated to complete compliance training annually.

---

### 496. own → possess

**Meaning:** to hold or have something, often formally or legally

**Examples:**
1. The company possesses exclusive rights to the patented process.
2. Each team member possesses the credentials needed for system access.

---

### 497. often → frequently

**Meaning:** happening many times, on a regular basis

**Examples:**
1. Customers frequently cite pricing as their top concern.
2. The dashboard is frequently updated to reflect live sales data.

---

### 498. over → exceed

**Meaning:** to go beyond a set limit or expectation

**Examples:**
1. Q2 revenue is projected to exceed our original forecast.
2. The project costs exceeded the approved budget by 12 percent.

---

### 499. out → excluded

**Meaning:** left out or not included in something

**Examples:**
1. Contractors are excluded from the standard benefits package.
2. Legacy systems were excluded from the migration scope.

---

### 500. only → exclusively

**Meaning:** solely, with nothing else included

**Examples:**
1. This offer is available exclusively to enterprise customers.
2. The dataset is used exclusively for internal reporting purposes.

---

### 501. open → commence

**Meaning:** to formally begin or start

**Examples:**
1. The audit will commence next Monday morning.
2. Negotiations are set to commence once due diligence is complete.

---

### 502. offer → propose

**Meaning:** to formally suggest an idea, plan, or deal

**Examples:**
1. The vendor proposed a revised pricing structure for renewal.
2. We propose a phased rollout to minimize operational risk.

---

### 503. order → sequence

**Meaning:** the specific arrangement in which things occur

**Examples:**
1. The implementation plan outlines the sequence of milestones.
2. Tasks should be completed in the sequence listed in the runbook.

---

### 504. old → legacy

**Meaning:** outdated, often referring to systems still in use

**Examples:**
1. The legacy infrastructure will be decommissioned next year.
2. Migrating data from the legacy platform is our top priority.

---

### 505. number → quantify

**Meaning:** to express something as a specific, measurable figure

**Examples:**
1. We need to quantify the potential impact of the outage.
2. The team was asked to quantify projected cost savings.

---

### 506. name → designate

**Meaning:** to formally assign a title, role, or category

**Examples:**
1. The board designated a new interim CEO on Friday.
2. Each risk was designated a priority level in the report.

---

### 507. level → tier

**Meaning:** a defined rank or layer within a structured system

**Examples:**
1. Enterprise customers receive a higher support tier.
2. The pricing model includes three distinct service tiers.

---

### 508. large → substantial

**Meaning:** significant in size, amount, or importance

**Examples:**
1. The merger represents a substantial investment for both firms.
2. We saw a substantial increase in engagement after the redesign.

---

### 509. last → final

**Meaning:** the last one in a sequence, with no more to follow

**Examples:**
1. This is the final draft before it goes to the client.
2. The final decision rests with the executive committee.

---

### 510. late → delayed

**Meaning:** occurring later than planned or expected

**Examples:**
1. The shipment was delayed due to customs clearance issues.
2. Delayed approvals pushed the launch date back two weeks.

---

### 511. long → extended

**Meaning:** lasting for a greater length of time than usual

**Examples:**
1. We negotiated an extended warranty for the equipment.
2. The vendor granted an extended payment period.

---

### 512. low → nominal

**Meaning:** very small, especially in terms of cost or amount

**Examples:**
1. The setup fee is nominal compared to long-term savings.
2. We saw only a nominal change in customer churn this quarter.

---

### 513. lead → spearhead

**Meaning:** to take charge of and drive an initiative forward

**Examples:**
1. She was asked to spearhead the digital transformation project.
2. The product team will spearhead the new feature rollout.

---

### 514. money → capital

**Meaning:** financial resources used to fund operations or growth

**Examples:**
1. The startup raised additional capital in its Series B round.
2. We need to allocate capital toward market expansion.

---

### 515. month → fiscal month

**Meaning:** a defined one-month accounting period used for reporting

**Examples:**
1. Revenue is reconciled at the close of each fiscal month.
2. The variance report is generated for every fiscal month.

---

### 516. less → reduced

**Meaning:** smaller in amount than before

**Examples:**
1. The new process resulted in reduced turnaround times.
2. We negotiated a reduced rate for the annual contract.

---

### 517. like → prefer

**Meaning:** to favor one option over others

**Examples:**
1. Most clients prefer a quarterly billing cycle.
2. The committee prefers the second vendor proposal.

---

### 518. let → permit

**Meaning:** to formally allow something to happen

**Examples:**
1. The updated policy permits remote work two days a week.
2. The license permits limited redistribution of the software.

---

### 519. live → operational

**Meaning:** fully functioning and in active use

**Examples:**
1. The new payment gateway is now fully operational.
2. All regional offices are expected to be operational by March.

---

### 520. loss → deficit

**Meaning:** the amount by which spending exceeds income

**Examples:**
1. The division reported a deficit in the last fiscal year.
2. Management is working to close the budget deficit.

---

### 521. lose → forfeit

**Meaning:** to lose something as a consequence of an action or rule

**Examples:**
1. Late applicants will forfeit their eligibility for the bonus.
2. The vendor will forfeit the deposit if the deadline is missed.

---

### 522. likely → probable

**Meaning:** expected to happen based on current information

**Examples:**
1. A probable outcome of the delay is a revised launch date.
2. Analysts consider a market slowdown probable this quarter.

---

### 523. leave → depart

**Meaning:** to exit a role, place, or organization

**Examples:**
1. Two senior directors are set to depart the company this year.
2. Employees must complete an exit interview before they depart.

---

### 524. launch → initiate

**Meaning:** to formally start a process, project, or campaign

**Examples:**
1. The team will initiate the pilot program next Monday.
2. We initiated the vendor selection process last week.

---

### 525. listen → heed

**Meaning:** to pay close attention to and act on advice or feedback

**Examples:**
1. Leadership should heed the concerns raised in the survey.
2. The team heeded the auditor's recommendations promptly.

---

### 526. mark → denote

**Meaning:** to indicate or signify something clearly

**Examples:**
1. A red flag in the system denotes a high-priority ticket.
2. The asterisk denotes mandatory fields on the form.

---

### 527. match → align

**Meaning:** to bring into agreement or correspondence with something

**Examples:**
1. We need to align our roadmap with the client's expectations.
2. The new policy aligns with industry compliance standards.

---

### 528. manage → administer

**Meaning:** to organize and oversee the execution of something

**Examples:**
1. HR will administer the annual performance review process.
2. The vendor administers our cloud infrastructure contracts.

---

### 529. model → framework

**Meaning:** a structured approach or system used to guide decisions

**Examples:**
1. We adopted a new framework for evaluating vendor risk.
2. The strategy is built on a well-tested pricing framework.

---

### 530. method → methodology

**Meaning:** a structured, systematic approach to doing something

**Examples:**
1. The agile methodology improved our sprint delivery times.
2. Our research methodology was outlined in the appendix.

---

### 531. matter → material

**Meaning:** significant enough to influence a decision or outcome

**Examples:**
1. This is a material risk that must be disclosed to investors.
2. Any material change to the contract requires legal review.

---

### 532. might → conceivably

**Meaning:** possibly, in a way that could reasonably happen

**Examples:**
1. The rollout could conceivably slip if testing uncovers issues.
2. Costs could conceivably rise if the supplier raises prices.

---

### 533. major → significant

**Meaning:** important or notable in scale or impact

**Examples:**
1. The update introduces a significant improvement in load times.
2. There was a significant shift in customer sentiment this quarter.

---

### 534. message → communication

**Meaning:** information conveyed from one party to another

**Examples:**
1. Please ensure the communication is approved before it goes external.
2. Internal communications should reflect the updated branding guidelines.

---

### 535. monitor → oversee

**Meaning:** to supervise and keep track of an ongoing process

**Examples:**
1. The compliance officer will oversee the audit process.
2. A dedicated team oversees vendor performance throughout the contract.

---

### 536. mix → integrate

**Meaning:** to combine elements so they function as one system

**Examples:**
1. We plan to integrate the acquired company's tools with ours.
2. The new platform integrates sales and support data seamlessly.

---

### 537. mistake → oversight

**Meaning:** an unintentional error, often due to a lapse in attention

**Examples:**
1. The delay was caused by an administrative oversight.
2. We identified an oversight in the original budget calculation.

---

### 538. maybe → potentially

**Meaning:** possibly, though not certainly

**Examples:**
1. The merger could potentially close before year end.
2. This approach could potentially reduce operating costs.

---

### 539. meaning → significance

**Meaning:** the importance or implication of something

**Examples:**
1. The board discussed the significance of the latest earnings report.
2. Analysts debated the significance of the new regulation.

---

### 540. limit → constrain

**Meaning:** to restrict or place boundaries on something

**Examples:**
1. Budget cuts will constrain our hiring plans this year.
2. Regulatory requirements constrain how the data can be used.

---

### 541. link → correlate

**Meaning:** to show a meaningful connection between two things

**Examples:**
1. Analysts found that ad spend correlates with signup growth.
2. The report shows employee satisfaction correlates with retention.

---

### 542. negative → unfavorable

**Meaning:** not favorable or beneficial to an outcome

**Examples:**
1. The audit revealed several unfavorable findings.
2. Unfavorable market conditions delayed the IPO.

---

### 543. notice → observe

**Meaning:** to become aware of something through attention or inspection

**Examples:**
1. The team observed a decline in engagement after the update.
2. Auditors observed inconsistencies in the expense reports.

---

### 544. normal → standard

**Meaning:** usual or expected, following an established norm

**Examples:**
1. This is the standard onboarding process for new vendors.
2. Response times remained within the standard SLA range.

---

### 545. near → imminent

**Meaning:** about to happen very soon

**Examples:**
1. With approvals complete, launch is now imminent.
2. The board expects an imminent decision on the acquisition.

---

### 546. nice → favorable

**Meaning:** producing a positive or advantageous result

**Examples:**
1. We negotiated favorable terms with the new supplier.
2. Favorable market conditions boosted quarterly revenue.

---

### 547. narrow → constrained

**Meaning:** limited in scope or range

**Examples:**
1. The project timeline is constrained by regulatory deadlines.
2. Resources remain constrained until the next funding round.

---

### 548. nothing → negligible

**Meaning:** so small as to be not worth considering

**Examples:**
1. The system update had a negligible effect on load times.
2. Customer complaints were negligible following the redesign.

---

### 549. natural → inherent

**Meaning:** existing as a basic or essential feature of something

**Examples:**
1. There is an inherent risk in entering a new market.
2. The role carries an inherent level of cross-team coordination.

---

### 550. notify → apprise

**Meaning:** to formally inform someone of a situation

**Examples:**
1. Please apprise the client of any changes to the delivery date.
2. Managers were apprised of the reorganization before the announcement.

---

### 551. necessary → imperative

**Meaning:** absolutely essential and requiring urgent attention

**Examples:**
1. It is imperative that we resolve this before the audit.
2. Timely communication with stakeholders is imperative.

---

### 552. neutral → impartial

**Meaning:** not favoring any side, fair and unbiased

**Examples:**
1. An impartial third party was brought in to mediate the dispute.
2. The review board remained impartial throughout the selection process.

---

### 553. numerous → multitude

**Meaning:** a very large number of something

**Examples:**
1. The proposal addresses a multitude of stakeholder concerns.
2. There are a multitude of factors driving the price increase.

---

### 554. outline → delineate

**Meaning:** to clearly describe or define the boundaries of something

**Examples:**
1. The contract delineates each party's responsibilities.
2. Please delineate the scope of work before we proceed.

---

### 555. overall → comprehensive

**Meaning:** complete and covering all necessary aspects

**Examples:**
1. We conducted a comprehensive review of the vendor's performance.
2. The comprehensive plan addresses both short and long-term goals.

---

### 556. obvious → evident

**Meaning:** clearly seen or understood without needing explanation

**Examples:**
1. It was evident from the data that demand had shifted.
2. The benefits of the new tool became evident within weeks.

---

### 557. outcome → yield

**Meaning:** the result or return produced by an effort or investment

**Examples:**
1. The training program is expected to yield measurable productivity gains.
2. Early trials yielded promising results for the new formula.

---

### 558. outside → external

**Meaning:** coming from or existing beyond the organization itself

**Examples:**
1. We brought in external consultants to review the process.
2. External factors, including inflation, affected our margins.

---

### 559. ongoing → continuous

**Meaning:** happening constantly without interruption

**Examples:**
1. We maintain continuous monitoring of system uptime.
2. Continuous feedback from customers shapes our roadmap.

---

### 560. okay → acceptable

**Meaning:** meeting the required or expected standard

**Examples:**
1. The proposed timeline is acceptable to the client.
2. An acceptable margin of error was defined before testing began.

---

### 561. odd → anomalous

**Meaning:** differing from what is normal or expected

**Examples:**
1. The audit flagged an anomalous spike in expenses last quarter.
2. Engineers investigated the anomalous drop in server traffic.

---

### 562. opinion → perspective

**Meaning:** a particular point of view or way of considering something

**Examples:**
1. We gathered leadership's perspective before finalizing the strategy.
2. The consultant offered a fresh perspective on our pricing model.

---

### 563. oversee → supervise

**Meaning:** to direct and be responsible for a process or team

**Examples:**
1. The regional manager will supervise the store rollout.
2. A dedicated project lead supervises all vendor deliverables.

---

### 564. occasional → intermittent

**Meaning:** happening at irregular intervals, not constant

**Examples:**
1. The system experienced intermittent connectivity issues last week.
2. Intermittent delays were reported across the supply chain.

---

### 565. optional → discretionary

**Meaning:** left to individual choice rather than required

**Examples:**
1. Attendance at the workshop is discretionary for senior staff.
2. The bonus is discretionary and based on annual performance.

---

### 566. ordinary → conventional

**Meaning:** following established or traditional norms

**Examples:**
1. We moved away from conventional advertising toward influencer marketing.
2. Conventional financing options were too slow for this deal.

---

### 567. overdue → outstanding

**Meaning:** not yet paid, completed, or resolved as required

**Examples:**
1. Please follow up on the outstanding invoice from last month.
2. Several action items remain outstanding from the last review.

---

### 568. outdated → obsolete

**Meaning:** no longer useful or in current use

**Examples:**
1. The obsolete software poses a growing security risk.
2. We are phasing out obsolete equipment across all facilities.

---

### 569. onset → inception

**Meaning:** the beginning or start of something

**Examples:**
1. Costs have tripled since the project's inception.
2. The partnership has grown steadily since its inception in 2019.

---

### 570. loyal → committed

**Meaning:** firmly dedicated to a person, brand, or cause

**Examples:**
1. Our most committed customers drive the majority of referrals.
2. The team remained committed despite the tight deadline.

---

### 571. lag → delay

**Meaning:** a period of time by which something is held back

**Examples:**
1. There is typically a delay between signup and first purchase.
2. Supply chain delays affected our product launch date.

---

### 572. layer → stratum

**Meaning:** a distinct level within a hierarchical structure

**Examples:**
1. Each stratum of management has its own reporting requirements.
2. The proposal targets a specific stratum of enterprise clients.

---

### 573. loop → circulate

**Meaning:** to share information among a group of people

**Examples:**
1. Please circulate the meeting notes to the wider team.
2. The draft proposal was circulated for feedback before submission.

---

### 574. load → capacity

**Meaning:** the maximum amount a system, team, or resource can handle

**Examples:**
1. The support team is currently operating near full capacity.
2. We need to assess server capacity before the product launch.

---

### 575. lucky → fortuitous

**Meaning:** happening by a favorable chance

**Examples:**
1. The timing of the deal was fortuitous given the market shift.
2. A fortuitous introduction led to our largest client partnership.

---

### 576. lots → abundant

**Meaning:** existing in large quantities

**Examples:**
1. There is abundant evidence supporting the proposed strategy.
2. The region offers an abundant supply of skilled talent.

---

### 577. lively → dynamic

**Meaning:** energetic and constantly changing or active

**Examples:**
1. The team fostered a dynamic culture of continuous improvement.
2. We operate in a dynamic market that demands rapid adaptation.

---

### 578. loose → flexible

**Meaning:** adaptable and not rigidly fixed

**Examples:**
1. The contract includes flexible terms for early termination.
2. We adopted a flexible work policy after the pilot program.

---

### 579. lasting → enduring

**Meaning:** continuing to exist over a long period of time

**Examples:**
1. The merger created an enduring partnership between both firms.
2. We aim to build enduring relationships with our key suppliers.

---

### 580. lighten → alleviate

**Meaning:** to reduce the severity or burden of something

**Examples:**
1. The new software should alleviate the team's administrative workload.
2. Outsourcing payroll helped alleviate pressure on HR staff.

---

### 581. leftover → residual

**Meaning:** remaining after the main part has been used or completed

**Examples:**
1. The residual budget will be allocated to next quarter's initiatives.
2. Residual risk remains even after implementing new controls.

---

### 582. local → regional

**Meaning:** relating to a specific defined area or district

**Examples:**
1. Regional sales teams report directly to the VP of Sales.
2. The policy varies slightly across our regional offices.

---

### 583. lack → deficiency

**Meaning:** an absence or shortage of something needed

**Examples:**
1. The audit identified a deficiency in our internal controls.
2. A deficiency in staffing delayed the project's completion.

---

### 584. learn → acquire

**Meaning:** to gain knowledge or skill through effort or experience

**Examples:**
1. New hires acquire product knowledge through a structured onboarding program.
2. The team acquired new certifications ahead of the compliance deadline.

---

### 585. leader → frontrunner

**Meaning:** the person or company currently in the leading position

**Examples:**
1. The company remains the frontrunner in the regional market.
2. She emerged as the frontrunner for the VP position.

---

### 586. light → trivial

**Meaning:** of little importance or significance

**Examples:**
1. The feedback raised only trivial concerns about the design.
2. Most of the requested changes were trivial edits to the copy.

---

### 587. lower → diminish

**Meaning:** to reduce in amount, size, or degree

**Examples:**
1. Automation helped diminish processing time by half.
2. The recall is expected to diminish consumer confidence temporarily.

---

### 588. lift → elevate

**Meaning:** to raise the level or status of something

**Examples:**
1. The rebrand helped elevate the company's market perception.
2. We aim to elevate customer experience across all touchpoints.

---

### 589. line → pipeline

**Meaning:** the sequence of prospects or projects currently in progress

**Examples:**
1. The sales pipeline looks strong heading into Q4.
2. We have several new features in the development pipeline.

---

### 590. landmark → watershed

**Meaning:** a critical turning point that leads to major change

**Examples:**
1. The acquisition marked a watershed moment for the company.
2. The new regulation represents a watershed shift for the industry.

---

### 591. largely → predominantly

**Meaning:** mostly or mainly, to a great extent

**Examples:**
1. Growth was predominantly driven by international markets.
2. The delay was predominantly due to supplier shortages.

---

### 592. lately → latterly

**Meaning:** recently, in a period close to the present

**Examples:**
1. Latterly, customer preferences have shifted toward subscription models.
2. Latterly, the team has focused more on retention than acquisition.

---

### 593. logical → rational

**Meaning:** based on sound reasoning rather than emotion

**Examples:**
1. A rational assessment of the risks led us to delay the launch.
2. The board made a rational decision to diversify suppliers.

---

### 594. modern → contemporary

**Meaning:** belonging to the present time, up to date

**Examples:**
1. The office adopted a contemporary approach to hybrid work.
2. Our contemporary marketing strategy relies heavily on data.

---

### 595. modify → amend

**Meaning:** to make a formal change to something

**Examples:**
1. We need to amend the contract to reflect the new terms.
2. The policy was amended following employee feedback.

---

### 596. mature → developed

**Meaning:** fully established, especially referring to a market or process

**Examples:**
1. We are expanding into more developed markets this year.
2. Our processes are now developed enough to scale internationally.

---

### 597. mild → moderate

**Meaning:** neither too strong nor too weak; in between extremes

**Examples:**
1. Analysts expect only a moderate decline in quarterly earnings.
2. The proposal received a moderate level of investor interest.

---

### 598. massive → considerable

**Meaning:** large in size or degree, notably significant

**Examples:**
1. The acquisition required considerable due diligence.
2. There was considerable investment in the new product line.

---

### 599. marginal → incremental

**Meaning:** relating to small, gradual changes or improvements

**Examples:**
1. The update delivered incremental improvements to load speed.
2. We expect incremental gains in efficiency each quarter.

---

### 600. manual → rudimentary

**Meaning:** basic and not yet automated or refined

**Examples:**
1. The current process is still fairly rudimentary and needs automation.
2. We replaced the rudimentary spreadsheet system with a proper CRM.

---

### 601. moment → juncture

**Meaning:** a particular point in time, often significant

**Examples:**
1. At this juncture, we need to reassess our vendor strategy.
2. The company is at a critical juncture in its growth story.

---

### 602. mostly → primarily

**Meaning:** mainly or for the most part

**Examples:**
1. Revenue growth was primarily driven by new customer acquisition.
2. The delay was primarily caused by regulatory approval timelines.

---

### 603. monthly → recurring

**Meaning:** happening repeatedly on a regular schedule

**Examples:**
1. We introduced a recurring billing option for subscribers.
2. The team holds a recurring sync to review project status.

---

### 604. multiple → manifold

**Meaning:** many and varied in kind

**Examples:**
1. The rollout involved manifold technical and logistical challenges.
2. There are manifold benefits to consolidating our vendor contracts.

---

### 605. motive → rationale

**Meaning:** the underlying reason behind a decision or action

**Examples:**
1. The board requested a clear rationale for the budget cut.
2. The rationale for the restructuring was outlined in the memo.

---

### 606. meaningful → substantive

**Meaning:** having real importance or containing significant content

**Examples:**
1. The workshop led to a substantive discussion on strategy.
2. We need substantive changes, not just cosmetic ones.

---

### 607. minimal → negligible

**Meaning:** so small it barely counts or matters

**Examples:**
1. The price change had a negligible impact on sales volume.
2. Downtime during the migration was negligible.

---

### 608. morale → engagement

**Meaning:** the level of enthusiasm and commitment employees feel toward their work

**Examples:**
1. The survey showed a noticeable dip in employee engagement.
2. Leadership introduced new initiatives to boost engagement.

---

### 609. measure → gauge

**Meaning:** to assess or estimate the extent of something

**Examples:**
1. We use quarterly surveys to gauge customer satisfaction.
2. The pilot program helped gauge demand before a full rollout.

---

### 610. merge → consolidate

**Meaning:** to combine multiple things into a single, unified whole

**Examples:**
1. The two departments will consolidate under a single director.
2. We plan to consolidate our vendor contracts to reduce costs.

---

### 611. miss → overlook

**Meaning:** to fail to notice or consider something

**Examples:**
1. The initial audit overlooked a key compliance requirement.
2. Don't overlook the shipping costs when comparing vendor quotes.

---

### 612. mention → reference

**Meaning:** to refer to something briefly in speech or writing

**Examples:**
1. The report references last year's audit findings for context.
2. Please reference the updated policy in your onboarding materials.

---

### 613. minor → immaterial

**Meaning:** not significant enough to affect the outcome

**Examples:**
1. The discrepancy was deemed immaterial by the auditors.
2. Most of the feedback involved immaterial formatting issues.

---

### 614. motivate → incentivize

**Meaning:** to encourage action through rewards or benefits

**Examples:**
1. The bonus structure is designed to incentivize early renewals.
2. We incentivized the sales team with a quarterly performance award.

---

### 615. noticeable → perceptible

**Meaning:** able to be detected or noticed, even if slight

**Examples:**
1. There was a perceptible improvement in response times after the fix.
2. Customers reported a perceptible difference in service quality.

---

### 616. narrowly → marginally

**Meaning:** by a very small amount or margin

**Examples:**
1. The proposal marginally exceeded the approved budget.
2. Revenue marginally missed the quarterly target.

---

### 617. nearby → proximate

**Meaning:** located close to a particular point, often in time or space

**Examples:**
1. We selected a proximate warehouse to reduce delivery times.
2. The proximate cause of the outage was a failed server update.

---

### 618. newly → recently

**Meaning:** happening or done a short time ago

**Examples:**
1. The recently appointed CFO is reviewing all major contracts.
2. Recently onboarded clients receive a dedicated account manager.

---

### 619. nonstop → uninterrupted

**Meaning:** continuing without any breaks or pauses

**Examples:**
1. We guarantee uninterrupted service during the migration window.
2. The system delivered uninterrupted uptime throughout the launch.

---

### 620. notable → prominent

**Meaning:** important and widely recognized

**Examples:**
1. The firm secured several prominent clients this quarter.
2. A prominent industry analyst praised the company's new strategy.

---

### 621. nowadays → currently

**Meaning:** at the present time

**Examples:**
1. Currently, most transactions are processed through the mobile app.
2. The team is currently evaluating three shortlisted vendors.

---

### 622. nimble → agile

**Meaning:** able to move or adapt quickly and easily

**Examples:**
1. The startup's agile team shipped the feature in under two weeks.
2. We restructured to become a more agile organization.

---

### 623. native → innate

**Meaning:** existing naturally within someone or something from the start

**Examples:**
1. The candidate showed an innate ability to manage client relationships.
2. The tool has an innate advantage due to its built-in analytics.

---

### 624. nervous → apprehensive

**Meaning:** feeling anxious or worried about an upcoming outcome

**Examples:**
1. Investors remained apprehensive ahead of the earnings call.
2. The team was apprehensive about the tight rollout schedule.

---

### 625. neat → elegant

**Meaning:** cleverly simple and well designed

**Examples:**
1. The engineering team proposed an elegant fix for the bottleneck.
2. The pricing model offers an elegant solution to a complex problem.

---

### 626. negatively → adversely

**Meaning:** in a way that causes harm or a bad effect

**Examples:**
1. Supply shortages adversely affected our production schedule.
2. The outage adversely impacted customer trust in the short term.

---

### 627. nonexistent → absent

**Meaning:** not present at all

**Examples:**
1. Formal documentation was largely absent from the handover.
2. Competitive pressure was virtually absent in this niche market.

---

### 628. novice → neophyte

**Meaning:** a person new to a role, skill, or field

**Examples:**
1. The mentorship program pairs neophyte analysts with senior staff.
2. As a neophyte in the industry, she relied heavily on her advisors.

---

### 629. nurture → cultivate

**Meaning:** to help something grow or develop over time

**Examples:**
1. Account managers work to cultivate long-term client relationships.
2. The program is designed to cultivate leadership skills in new hires.

---

### 630. nix → rescind

**Meaning:** to officially cancel or withdraw a decision or offer

**Examples:**
1. The company decided to rescind the job offer after the background check.
2. Management rescinded the policy following widespread employee feedback.

---

## Section: P – R

### 631. put → implement

**Meaning:** to put a plan or system into action

**Examples:**
1. We will implement the new onboarding process next quarter.
2. The team implemented several cost-saving measures this year.

---

### 632. plan → strategize

**Meaning:** to develop a careful approach for achieving a goal

**Examples:**
1. Leadership will strategize the market entry over the next two weeks.
2. We need to strategize before pitching to investors.

---

### 633. pay → compensate

**Meaning:** to pay someone for their work, time, or a loss

**Examples:**
1. The company compensates employees fairly for overtime.
2. We compensated the vendor for the delayed shipment.

---

### 634. price → appraise

**Meaning:** to assess the value or cost of something

**Examples:**
1. Our team appraised the property before the acquisition.
2. The consultant appraised the startup's assets prior to sale.

---

### 635. part → component

**Meaning:** an individual piece that makes up a larger whole

**Examples:**
1. Marketing is a key component of our growth strategy.
2. Each component of the proposal was reviewed separately.

---

### 636. place → allocate

**Meaning:** to set aside resources for a specific purpose

**Examples:**
1. We allocated additional budget to the design team.
2. Management allocated three engineers to the new project.

---

### 637. problem → challenge

**Meaning:** a difficult issue that needs to be addressed

**Examples:**
1. Supply chain delays remain a significant challenge for us.
2. The team identified several challenges in the current workflow.

---

### 638. people → personnel

**Meaning:** the staff or workforce of an organization

**Examples:**
1. HR is responsible for all personnel matters.
2. We need additional personnel to meet the deadline.

---

### 639. product → offering

**Meaning:** something a company sells or provides to customers

**Examples:**
1. Our latest offering targets small business owners.
2. The company expanded its offering to include consulting services.

---

### 640. power → authority

**Meaning:** the official right to make decisions

**Examples:**
1. The director has the authority to approve this budget.
2. She was granted authority over the regional operations.

---

### 641. point → objective

**Meaning:** a specific goal one aims to achieve

**Examples:**
1. The main objective of the meeting is to finalize the budget.
2. Our objective this quarter is to reduce churn.

---

### 642. prove → demonstrate

**Meaning:** to show clearly that something is true or effective

**Examples:**
1. The pilot program demonstrated a clear return on investment.
2. We need data to demonstrate the strategy's success.

---

### 643. push → drive

**Meaning:** to actively work to make something happen

**Examples:**
1. Leadership is driving adoption of the new software.
2. The sales team drove significant growth this quarter.

---

### 644. pick → select

**Meaning:** to choose from a group of options

**Examples:**
1. We selected three vendors for the final review.
2. The committee will select a candidate by Friday.

---

### 645. promise → commit

**Meaning:** to formally agree to do something

**Examples:**
1. The vendor committed to a two-week turnaround.
2. We committed to reducing costs by ten percent.

---

### 646. personal → bespoke

**Meaning:** custom-made or tailored to individual needs

**Examples:**
1. We offer a bespoke onboarding experience for enterprise clients.
2. The consultant provided bespoke recommendations for each department.

---

### 647. private → confidential

**Meaning:** intended to be kept secret within a limited group

**Examples:**
1. This information is confidential and should not be shared externally.
2. We discussed confidential matters during the board meeting.

---

### 648. public → transparent

**Meaning:** open and visible to everyone, not hidden

**Examples:**
1. The company committed to transparent reporting practices.
2. We aim to be transparent about our pricing structure.

---

### 649. poor → subpar

**Meaning:** below an acceptable standard

**Examples:**
1. The vendor's subpar performance led us to seek alternatives.
2. Customer satisfaction dropped due to subpar service quality.

---

### 650. possible → feasible

**Meaning:** able to be done practically

**Examples:**
1. The engineering team confirmed the timeline is feasible.
2. We assessed whether expansion into Asia was feasible.

---

### 651. popular → prevalent

**Meaning:** widely used or common among a group

**Examples:**
1. Remote work has become prevalent across the industry.
2. This payment method is prevalent among younger customers.

---

### 652. present → showcase

**Meaning:** to display something to highlight its value

**Examples:**
1. We will showcase our new product line at the conference.
2. The team showcased quarterly results to the board.

---

### 653. provide → furnish

**Meaning:** to supply someone with something needed

**Examples:**
1. The vendor will furnish all necessary documentation.
2. We furnished the client with a detailed cost breakdown.

---

### 654. protect → safeguard

**Meaning:** to keep something secure from harm or loss

**Examples:**
1. The new policy safeguards customer data.
2. We implemented controls to safeguard company assets.

---

### 655. prevent → mitigate

**Meaning:** to reduce the severity or likelihood of a problem

**Examples:**
1. These measures will mitigate the risk of delays.
2. We updated the protocol to mitigate security threats.

---

### 656. prepare → formulate

**Meaning:** to develop something carefully, such as a plan

**Examples:**
1. The team formulated a response to the client's concerns.
2. We are formulating next year's marketing strategy.

---

### 657. practice → protocol

**Meaning:** an established procedure for how something is done

**Examples:**
1. Please follow the standard protocol for expense reports.
2. We revised our onboarding protocol last month.

---

### 658. praise → commend

**Meaning:** to express approval or admiration for something

**Examples:**
1. The manager commended the team for hitting the target.
2. Clients commended our responsive customer service.

---

### 659. pretty → considerably

**Meaning:** to a significant degree

**Examples:**
1. Revenue grew considerably compared to last quarter.
2. The new process is considerably more efficient.

---

### 660. plain → straightforward

**Meaning:** simple and easy to understand

**Examples:**
1. The proposal outlines a straightforward implementation plan.
2. We prefer a straightforward pricing model for clients.

---

### 661. pass → approve

**Meaning:** to officially accept or authorize something

**Examples:**
1. The board approved the merger unanimously.
2. Finance must approve the budget before we proceed.

---

### 662. path → trajectory

**Meaning:** the direction something is heading over time

**Examples:**
1. The company is on a strong growth trajectory.
2. We adjusted our trajectory after the market shift.

---

### 663. pattern → trend

**Meaning:** a general direction in which something is developing

**Examples:**
1. We've noticed a trend of increasing customer churn.
2. Analysts identified an emerging trend in remote hiring.

---

### 664. permit → authorize

**Meaning:** to give official approval for something

**Examples:**
1. Only the director can authorize expenditures over $10,000.
2. The client authorized the additional scope of work.

---

### 665. persuade → influence

**Meaning:** to affect someone's decisions or opinions

**Examples:**
1. Our presentation influenced the client's final decision.
2. Market conditions influenced the board's strategy.

---

### 666. plenty → abundance

**Meaning:** a large amount of something

**Examples:**
1. There is an abundance of talent in this candidate pool.
2. The region offers an abundance of investment opportunities.

---

### 667. positive → favorable

**Meaning:** showing signs of success or good results

**Examples:**
1. We received favorable feedback from the pilot customers.
2. Market conditions remain favorable for expansion.

---

### 668. precise → granular

**Meaning:** detailed and specific, down to small components

**Examples:**
1. The report provides granular insight into regional sales.
2. We need granular data to identify the root cause.

---

### 669. priority → imperative

**Meaning:** something extremely important and urgent

**Examples:**
1. Reducing customer churn is our top imperative this year.
2. Data security has become a strategic imperative.

---

### 670. process → workflow

**Meaning:** the sequence of steps used to complete a task

**Examples:**
1. We streamlined the approval workflow to save time.
2. The onboarding workflow now takes half the time.

---

### 671. progress → momentum

**Meaning:** forward movement or advancement toward a goal

**Examples:**
1. The project has gained strong momentum this quarter.
2. We need to maintain momentum heading into the launch.

---

### 672. promote → champion

**Meaning:** to actively support and advocate for something

**Examples:**
1. She has championed diversity initiatives across the company.
2. The CEO champions a culture of continuous improvement.

---

### 673. prompt → expedite

**Meaning:** to make a process happen more quickly

**Examples:**
1. We expedited the shipment to meet the client's deadline.
2. Please expedite approval for this urgent request.

---

### 674. proper → appropriate

**Meaning:** suitable and fitting for a particular situation

**Examples:**
1. Ensure the appropriate documentation is attached to the invoice.
2. We followed the appropriate escalation procedure.

---

### 675. propose → recommend

**Meaning:** to formally suggest a course of action

**Examples:**
1. The consultant recommended restructuring the sales team.
2. We recommend adopting the new vendor for cost savings.

---

### 676. punish → penalize

**Meaning:** to impose a consequence for a violation

**Examples:**
1. The contract penalizes late delivery with a service credit.
2. Vendors are penalized for missing quality standards.

---

### 677. purpose → rationale

**Meaning:** the underlying reason for a decision or action

**Examples:**
1. The rationale behind the restructuring was cost efficiency.
2. Please include the rationale for this budget request.

---

### 678. pursue → undertake

**Meaning:** to begin and carry out a task or project

**Examples:**
1. We will undertake a full audit of our supply chain.
2. The firm undertook a major digital transformation initiative.

---

### 679. payment → remittance

**Meaning:** money sent to settle a debt or invoice

**Examples:**
1. Please confirm the remittance was received by the vendor.
2. The remittance for last month's invoice is still pending.

---

### 680. peak → zenith

**Meaning:** the highest point of success or activity

**Examples:**

2. Sales hit their zenith right before the product launch.

---

### 681. penalty → sanction

**Meaning:** an official punishment for breaking a rule or agreement

**Examples:**
1. The regulator imposed a sanction for the compliance breach.
2. Late payments incur a contractual sanction.

---

### 682. perfect → optimal

**Meaning:** the best possible under the circumstances

**Examples:**
1. We identified the optimal pricing strategy for the new market.
2. This is the optimal time to launch the campaign.

---

### 683. perform → execute

**Meaning:** to carry out a task or plan effectively

**Examples:**

2. We need to execute this strategy flawlessly.

### 684. period → timeframe

**Meaning:** a specific length of time

**Examples:**

2. We reviewed performance over the previous timeframe.

### 685. persist → sustain

**Meaning:** to keep something going over time

**Examples:**
1. We must sustain our efforts to retain top talent.
2. The company sustained growth despite market headwinds.

---

### 686. phase → stage

**Meaning:** a distinct period in a process

**Examples:**

### 687. pick up → accelerate

**Meaning:** to increase in speed or intensity

### 688. piece → segment

**Meaning:** a distinct part of a market, report, or process

### 689. pile → backlog

**Meaning:** a buildup of unfinished work or requests

### 690. plant → facility

**Meaning:** a building used for manufacturing or operations

### 691. play → contribute

**Meaning:** to have a part or influence in an outcome

---

### 692. please → satisfy

**Meaning:** to meet someone's needs or expectations

**Examples:**
1. Our goal is to satisfy client requirements ahead of deadline.

### 693. plot → devise

**Meaning:** to plan something carefully, often in detail

**Examples:**

### 694. point out → highlight

**Meaning:** to draw attention to something important

### 695. policy → guideline

**Meaning:** a set of rules or principles for how to act

**Examples:**
1. HR updated the remote work guideline last month.
