1. The CFO elucidated the assumptions behind the revenue forecast.
2. Please elucidate the new expense policy before the all-hands meeting.

---

### 1161. clash → friction

**Meaning:** conflict or disagreement between people or ideas

**Examples:**
1. There was noticeable friction between the sales and product teams over roadmap priorities.
2. The merger created friction between the two companies' cultures.

---

### 1162. class → caliber

**Meaning:** the level of quality or ability someone or something has

**Examples:**
1. We only hire candidates of the highest caliber for the leadership program.
2. The caliber of the presentation impressed every investor in the room.

---

### 1163. classic → quintessential

**Meaning:** representing the most perfect or typical example of something

**Examples:**
1. This launch is a quintessential example of how not to manage a product rollout.
2. The case study is quintessential for teaching negotiation strategy.

---

### 1164. click → resonate

**Meaning:** to connect with someone emotionally or make sense to them

**Examples:**
1. The new tagline really resonated with our younger customer segment.
2. The proposal didn't resonate with the board, so we're revising it.

---

### 1165. climate → landscape

**Meaning:** the general conditions or environment in a particular area, such as an industry

**Examples:**
1. The competitive landscape has shifted dramatically since the last review.
2. We need to reassess our strategy given the current regulatory landscape.

---

### 1166. climb → ascend

**Meaning:** to rise or move upward, e.g. in rank or amount

**Examples:**
1. Shares ascended sharply after the earnings beat expectations.
2. She ascended quickly from analyst to director in just three years.

---

### 1167. cling → adhere

**Meaning:** to stick firmly to a plan, rule, or belief

**Examples:**
1. All contractors must adhere to the updated safety protocols.
2. The team adhered closely to the original project timeline despite pressure to rush.

---

### 1168. clip → excerpt

**Meaning:** a short extract taken from a larger piece of content

**Examples:**
1. We included a short excerpt from the customer testimonial in the pitch deck.
2. Legal asked us to remove the excerpt from the confidential report.

---

### 1169. clock → log

**Meaning:** to record the occurrence or duration of something officially

**Examples:**
1. Please log your hours accurately for the client billing report.
2. The system automatically logs every login for security purposes.

---

### 1170. clone → replicate

**Meaning:** to copy something exactly, such as a process or product

**Examples:**
1. We plan to replicate the successful pilot program in three more regions.
2. Competitors quickly replicated our checkout flow after it launched.

---

### 1171. cloud → muddy

**Meaning:** to make something less clear or more confusing

**Examples:**
1. Adding too many metrics can muddy the key message of the report.
2. Conflicting feedback from stakeholders muddied the project's priorities.

---

### 1172. club → consortium

**Meaning:** a group of organizations or people collaborating for a common purpose

**Examples:**
1. Several universities formed a consortium to fund the joint research initiative.
2. The airline joined a consortium to develop shared sustainability standards.

---

### 1173. clue → inkling

**Meaning:** a slight suspicion or hint of something

**Examples:**
1. The rising complaint rate gave us an early inkling that the product had a flaw.
2. We had no inkling the competitor was about to announce a similar feature.

---

### 1174. cluster → concentration

**Meaning:** a close grouping of similar things in one area

**Examples:**
1. There is a concentration of clients in the manufacturing sector.
2. The startup ecosystem shows a concentration of activity in three major cities.

---

### 1175. clutch → pivotal

**Meaning:** extremely important, especially at a critical moment

**Examples:**
1. Her pivotal decision to renegotiate the contract saved the deal.
2. This quarter is pivotal for proving the new strategy actually works.

---

### 1176. coast → cruise

**Meaning:** to proceed smoothly and with little effort

**Examples:**
1. The team cruised through the audit thanks to strong documentation.
2. We can't afford to cruise through this quarter; targets are ambitious.

---

### 1177. code → protocol

**Meaning:** an official set of rules or procedures for how something should be done

**Examples:**
1. All staff must follow the data security protocol when handling customer records.
2. The new incident response protocol reduced downtime significantly.

---

### 1178. cold → indifferent

**Meaning:** showing no interest or concern

**Examples:**
1. The client's indifferent response to the proposal was a warning sign.
2. We noticed an indifferent attitude toward the new policy among senior staff.

---

### 1179. collapse → implode

**Meaning:** to fail or fall apart suddenly and completely, often from internal pressure

**Examples:**
1. The joint venture imploded after disagreements over profit sharing.
2. Analysts feared the deal would implode without stronger governance.

---

### 1180. colleague → associate

**Meaning:** a person one works with, especially in a professional capacity

**Examples:**
1. My associate in the London office will handle the client's local requirements.
2. We are hiring three new associates to support the growing account base.

---

## Section: D – F (More)

### 1181. daily → recurring

**Meaning:** happening again and again on a regular schedule

**Examples:**
1. We've set up a recurring sync to review pipeline numbers every morning.
2. The vendor invoice is a recurring charge that hits our account on the first of each month.

---

### 1182. debt → liability

**Meaning:** money or an obligation a company owes to others

**Examples:**
1. The finance team flagged the lease as a long-term liability on the balance sheet.
2. We need to restructure our liabilities before the next funding round.

---

### 1183. decline → ebb

**Meaning:** to gradually decrease or lose strength

**Examples:**
1. Customer engagement began to ebb after the promotional period ended.
2. If morale continues to ebb, we'll see it show up in attrition numbers.

---

### 1184. dedicate → allocate

**Meaning:** to officially set aside resources for a specific purpose

**Examples:**
1. Leadership agreed to allocate two additional engineers to the migration project.
2. We allocated a larger share of the marketing budget to paid social this quarter.

---

### 1185. defeat → surmount

**Meaning:** to successfully overcome a difficult obstacle

**Examples:**
1. The team surmounted several regulatory hurdles before launching in the new market.
2. With the right vendor support, we can surmount the integration challenges by Q3.

---

### 1186. defect → deficiency

**Meaning:** a flaw or shortcoming in something

**Examples:**
1. The audit uncovered a deficiency in our internal controls process.
2. Engineering traced the outage to a deficiency in the failover logic.

---

### 1187. defend → justify

**Meaning:** to give reasons that show a decision or action is right

**Examples:**
1. You'll need to justify the added headcount request to the executive committee.
2. The pricing change is hard to justify without stronger competitive data.

---

### 1188. deficit → shortfall

**Meaning:** the amount by which something is less than what is needed

**Examples:**
1. The division reported a revenue shortfall of twelve percent against target.
2. We're addressing the staffing shortfall by fast-tracking two open reqs.

---

### 1189. duplicate → replicate

**Meaning:** to copy or reproduce something exactly

**Examples:**
1. We plan to replicate this pilot's process across the other regional offices.
2. Can you replicate the bug in the staging environment before we ship a fix?

---

### 1190. delete → purge

**Meaning:** to remove something completely, often in bulk

**Examples:**
1. IT will purge inactive user accounts at the end of every quarter.
2. We need to purge outdated records before migrating to the new CRM.

---

### 1191. demote → relegate

**Meaning:** to move someone or something to a lower rank or position of importance

**Examples:**
1. The underperforming product line was relegated to a secondary priority.
2. He was relegated to an individual contributor role after the reorg.

---

### 1192. depart → withdraw

**Meaning:** to leave or pull out of a role, deal, or situation

**Examples:**
1. Our lead investor decided to withdraw from the funding round at the last minute.
2. She plans to withdraw from the project once the handover is complete.

---

### 1193. deploy → mobilize

**Meaning:** to organize and put resources into action quickly

**Examples:**
1. We mobilized a cross-functional task force to handle the outage.
2. The agency mobilized its full creative team ahead of the product launch.

---

### 1194. deposit → earmark

**Meaning:** to set money or resources aside for a specific use

**Examples:**
1. The board earmarked five million dollars for the new data center.
2. We've earmarked part of next year's budget for employee training.

---

### 1195. depress → suppress

**Meaning:** to hold something down or prevent it from rising

**Examples:**
1. Discount pricing can suppress margins if it isn't managed carefully.
2. Uncertainty in the market has suppressed consumer spending this quarter.

---

### 1196. deputy → delegate

**Meaning:** a person appointed to act or speak on behalf of someone else

**Examples:**
1. The CFO sent her delegate to represent finance at the vendor negotiation.
2. Each department nominated a delegate to sit on the steering committee.

---

### 1197. derive → extrapolate

**Meaning:** to work out a conclusion or estimate from existing data

**Examples:**
1. We extrapolated next year's demand from the last three quarters of sales data.
2. Analysts extrapolated growth trends from a limited early-adopter sample.

---

### 1198. descend → plummet

**Meaning:** to fall very quickly and sharply

**Examples:**
1. Shares plummeted after the earnings miss was announced.
2. Website traffic plummeted following the outage on launch day.

---

### 1199. deserve → merit

**Meaning:** to be worthy of or entitled to something

**Examples:**
1. This proposal merits a closer look from the investment committee.
2. Her performance this year merits a promotion, not just a bonus.

---

### 1200. dominate → prevail

**Meaning:** to win out over competitors or remain the strongest force

**Examples:**
1. Our platform has prevailed over three rival vendors in this segment.
2. We expect the incumbent brand to prevail unless we differentiate quickly.

---

### 1201. detach → segregate

**Meaning:** to separate something and keep it apart from the rest

**Examples:**
1. We segregated test data from production data to reduce compliance risk.
2. The finance team segregated duties between approval and payment processing.

---

### 1202. detain → withhold

**Meaning:** to hold something back and not release it

**Examples:**
1. Legal advised us to withhold the announcement until the filing is complete.
2. The client is withholding final payment pending sign-off on the deliverables.

---

### 1203. deteriorate → regress

**Meaning:** to move backward to a worse state after improving

**Examples:**
1. Response times regressed after the last deployment, so we rolled it back.
2. Client relations regressed once the account manager left the team.

---

### 1204. determine → ascertain

**Meaning:** to find out something for certain through investigation

**Examples:**
1. We're still working to ascertain the root cause of the data discrepancy.
2. Please ascertain whether the client has signed the updated contract.

---

### 1205. devastate → decimate

**Meaning:** to severely damage or reduce something in scale

**Examples:**
1. The supply chain disruption decimated our on-time delivery rate.
2. Aggressive discounting decimated the category's average margins.

---

### 1206. devote → channel

**Meaning:** to direct effort or resources toward a specific goal

**Examples:**
1. We're channeling most of our R&D budget into the mobile experience.
2. Leadership asked us to channel more energy into customer retention this year.

---

### 1207. diagram → schematic

**Meaning:** a detailed technical drawing showing how something is structured

**Examples:**
1. The architecture schematic outlines how each microservice communicates.
2. Attach a schematic of the new office layout to the facilities request.

---

### 1208. dictate → stipulate

**Meaning:** to state a requirement clearly as a condition

**Examples:**
1. The contract stipulates a thirty-day notice period for termination.
2. Our compliance policy stipulates that all vendors pass a security review.

---

### 1209. dread → abhor

**Meaning:** to strongly dislike or dread something

**Examples:**
1. Most managers abhor last-minute scope changes right before a deadline.
2. The team abhors unnecessary meetings that don't have a clear agenda.

---

### 1210. dilute → attenuate

**Meaning:** to weaken the strength or effect of something

**Examples:**
1. Adding too many features can attenuate the product's core value proposition.
2. Currency fluctuations attenuated the reported growth in overseas markets.

---

### 1211. donate → bestow

**Meaning:** to formally give something valuable to someone

**Examples:**
1. The company bestowed an innovation award on the winning team.
2. Our foundation bestows grants on select community education programs.

---

### 1212. disappear → dissipate

**Meaning:** to gradually fade away until nothing remains

**Examples:**
1. Early enthusiasm for the initiative dissipated once budgets got tight.
2. Concerns about the merger dissipated after leadership addressed them directly.

---

### 1213. disappoint → underwhelm

**Meaning:** to fail to meet expectations, leaving people unimpressed

**Examples:**
1. The quarterly results underwhelmed analysts who expected stronger growth.
2. Early user feedback suggests the new feature underwhelmed our beta testers.

---

### 1214. disapprove → denounce

**Meaning:** to strongly and publicly criticize something as wrong

**Examples:**
1. The regulator denounced the firm's disclosure practices in its report.
2. Several board members denounced the proposed executive pay package.

---

### 1215. disaster → calamity

**Meaning:** an event causing severe damage or major failure

**Examples:**
1. The failed product launch turned into a full-blown calamity for the brand.
2. A calamity like the server breach can undo years of customer trust.

---

### 1216. discard → jettison

**Meaning:** to get rid of something no longer needed or useful

**Examples:**
1. We decided to jettison the legacy reporting tool in favor of the new dashboard.
2. The team jettisoned two low-performing product lines from the roadmap.

---

### 1217. discharge → terminate

**Meaning:** to formally end someone's employment or a contract

**Examples:**
1. HR terminated the vendor agreement after repeated service failures.
2. The company terminated his contract following the compliance investigation.

---

### 1218. disclose → divulge

**Meaning:** to reveal information that was previously private

**Examples:**
1. We are not permitted to divulge financial details before the public filing.
2. The whistleblower divulged internal documents to the regulator.

---

### 1219. discount → undervalue

**Meaning:** to consider something as worth less than it actually is

**Examples:**
1. Investors may undervalue the company's recurring revenue potential.
2. Don't undervalue the impact of strong customer support on retention.

---

### 1220. discourage → deter

**Meaning:** to make someone less willing to do something

**Examples:**
1. High switching costs deter customers from moving to a competitor.
2. Strict compliance checks are meant to deter fraudulent claims.

---

### 1221. discredit → undermine

**Meaning:** to weaken trust or confidence in something

**Examples:**
1. The leaked memo undermined confidence in the leadership team.
2. Inconsistent messaging can undermine a brand's credibility over time.

---

### 1222. disguise → obscure

**Meaning:** to hide or make something unclear

**Examples:**
1. The revised report obscures the true impact of the cost overruns.
2. Complex fee structures can obscure the real price customers pay.

---

### 1223. dislike → disdain

**Meaning:** to have strong contempt or low regard for something

**Examples:**
1. The sales team disdains any process that adds friction to closing deals.
2. Customers have voiced disdain for the recent pricing changes.

---

### 1224. dismantle → deconstruct

**Meaning:** to take something apart piece by piece to examine it

**Examples:**
1. The consultants deconstructed our workflow to find where delays occur.
2. We deconstructed the competitor's pricing model to understand their strategy.

---

### 1225. disorder → disarray

**Meaning:** a state of confusion or lack of organization

**Examples:**
1. The handover left the project files in complete disarray.
2. The sudden departure of the manager threw the department into disarray.

---

### 1226. dispatch → expedite

**Meaning:** to speed up a process or send something quickly

**Examples:**
1. We're working with the courier to expedite delivery of the samples.
2. Can legal expedite the review so we can sign before month-end?

---

### 1227. dispel → quell

**Meaning:** to put an end to a feeling or rumor, making it go away

**Examples:**
1. The CEO's memo helped quell rumors about upcoming layoffs.
2. A clear roadmap quelled the team's concerns about project direction.

---

### 1228. displace → supplant

**Meaning:** to take the place of something, often forcing it out

**Examples:**
1. The new platform is expected to supplant our legacy system by year-end.
2. Automated tools have supplanted several manual reporting tasks.

---

### 1229. dispute → contest

**Meaning:** to formally challenge or disagree with a decision or claim

**Examples:**
1. The client intends to contest the invoice on the grounds of scope creep.
2. We're contesting the vendor's interpretation of the service agreement.

---

### 1230. distress → afflict

**Meaning:** to cause serious trouble or hardship

**Examples:**
1. Rising input costs continue to afflict manufacturers across the sector.
2. Staff shortages have afflicted the customer service department all summer.

---

### 1231. earnest → conscientious

**Meaning:** careful, thorough, and serious about doing something well

**Examples:**
1. Her conscientious approach to quality checks has cut our defect rate in half.
2. We need a conscientious reviewer to sign off on the final compliance report.

---

### 1232. edgy → provocative

**Meaning:** bold and intended to challenge or stir strong reactions

**Examples:**
1. The campaign's provocative tone generated a lot of social media discussion.
2. Legal flagged the provocative slogan as a potential brand risk.

---

### 1233. echo → reiterate

**Meaning:** to say something again to emphasize or confirm it

**Examples:**
1. I'd like to reiterate that the deadline for submissions is Friday.
2. The manager reiterated the company's commitment to remote work flexibility.

---

### 1234. economic → fiscal

**Meaning:** relating to money, budgets, or financial matters

**Examples:**
1. Our fiscal year ends in March, so budgets are due by February.
2. The fiscal outlook for the division improved after the cost-cutting measures.

---

### 1235. educate → apprise

**Meaning:** to formally inform someone of important details

**Examples:**
1. Please apprise the stakeholders of any changes to the rollout schedule.
2. We'll apprise the board of the audit findings at next week's meeting.

---

### 1236. elite → preeminent

**Meaning:** widely recognized as the best or most distinguished

**Examples:**
1. The firm is regarded as the preeminent advisor in cross-border mergers.
2. We hired a preeminent researcher to lead the new innovation lab.

---

### 1237. efficient → streamlined

**Meaning:** made simpler and faster by removing unnecessary steps

**Examples:**
1. The new approval workflow is far more streamlined than the old one.
2. We streamlined onboarding so new hires are productive within a week.

---

### 1238. elaborate → expound

**Meaning:** to explain something in greater detail

**Examples:**
1. Could you expound on the assumptions behind the revenue forecast?
2. The consultant expounded on the risks of delaying the migration.

---

### 1239. elegant → refined

**Meaning:** polished, sophisticated, and well executed

**Examples:**
1. The new interface feels far more refined than the previous version.
2. Their pitch deck had a refined, minimalist design that impressed investors.

---

### 1240. eliminate → eradicate

**Meaning:** to completely get rid of something

**Examples:**
1. We eradicated duplicate entries from the customer database last quarter.
2. The new checklist has helped eradicate most of the compliance errors.

---

### 1241. elude → circumvent

**Meaning:** to avoid or get around something cleverly

**Examples:**
1. The vendor tried to circumvent the approval process by going straight to the CEO.
2. We found a way to circumvent the licensing restriction without breaching terms.

---

### 1242. embarrass → mortify

**Meaning:** to cause someone to feel deeply ashamed or humiliated

**Examples:**
1. The public data breach mortified the entire executive team.
2. A typo in the press release mortified the communications department.

---

### 1243. embed → integrate

**Meaning:** to combine one thing fully into another so they work together

**Examples:**
1. We're integrating the payment gateway directly into the checkout flow.
2. The new hire quickly integrated into the cross-functional team.

---

### 1244. embrace → assimilate

**Meaning:** to fully take in and adopt something as part of the whole

**Examples:**
1. The company has worked hard to assimilate the acquired startup's culture.
2. New employees are expected to assimilate our core values quickly.

---

### 1245. emerge → materialize

**Meaning:** to become real or actually happen after being expected

**Examples:**
1. The partnership we discussed last quarter has finally materialized.
2. If the risks materialize, we'll need a contingency budget in place.

---

### 1246. emit → emanate

**Meaning:** to come out from a source, often gradually

**Examples:**
1. A sense of confidence emanated from the team during the investor pitch.
2. Complaints emanating from the support desk pointed to a bigger product issue.

---

### 1247. emphasize → underscore

**Meaning:** to highlight the importance of something

**Examples:**
1. The report underscores the need for faster decision-making at the executive level.
2. Let's underscore our commitment to sustainability in the annual report.

---

### 1248. empower → authorize

**Meaning:** to formally give someone the power or permission to act

**Examples:**
1. The board authorized the CFO to negotiate the acquisition terms.
2. Managers are authorized to approve expenses up to five thousand dollars.

---

### 1249. enclose → append

**Meaning:** to attach something additional to a document or message

**Examples:**
1. Please append the signed NDA to your reply before we proceed.
2. I've appended the revised budget as an attachment to this email.

---

### 1250. encounter → confront

**Meaning:** to face and deal directly with a problem or challenge

**Examples:**
1. We'll need to confront the staffing gap before scaling the operation.
2. The rollout confronted several compatibility issues in the first week.

---

### 1251. endanger → jeopardize

**Meaning:** to put something at serious risk of harm or failure

**Examples:**
1. Missing this deadline could jeopardize the entire product launch.
2. Cutting the QA budget jeopardizes the reliability of the release.

---

### 1252. endorse → ratify

**Meaning:** to formally approve or confirm an agreement or decision

**Examples:**
1. The shareholders are expected to ratify the merger at Thursday's vote.
2. The committee ratified the new expense policy without amendments.

---

### 1253. endure → withstand

**Meaning:** to survive difficult conditions without giving way

**Examples:**
1. Our supply chain withstood the disruption better than most competitors.
2. The platform is built to withstand sudden spikes in user traffic.

---

### 1254. energetic → dynamic

**Meaning:** full of energy and able to adapt quickly to change

**Examples:**
1. We're looking for a dynamic leader to take the regional team forward.
2. The startup's dynamic culture attracts candidates who like fast-moving work.

---

### 1255. enforce → mandate

**Meaning:** to require something officially and make sure it is followed

**Examples:**
1. The new policy mandates two-factor authentication for all employees.
2. Compliance will mandate quarterly training for anyone handling client data.

---

### 1256. engineer → orchestrate

**Meaning:** to carefully plan and coordinate a complex activity

**Examples:**
1. The operations team orchestrated a seamless transition to the new warehouse.
2. Marketing orchestrated a coordinated launch across five channels at once.

---

### 1257. enhance → augment

**Meaning:** to make something greater by adding to it

**Examples:**
1. The new analytics module augments our existing reporting suite.
2. We're hiring contractors to augment the team during peak season.

---

### 1258. enlarge → amplify

**Meaning:** to increase the size, strength, or effect of something

**Examples:**
1. Paid promotion will amplify the reach of our organic content.
2. The partnership amplifies our presence in markets we haven't entered yet.

---

### 1259. enlist → recruit

**Meaning:** to persuade someone to join and help with an effort

**Examples:**
1. We recruited three former clients to serve on the advisory board.
2. The project lead recruited volunteers from other teams to hit the deadline.

---

### 1260. enormous → substantial

**Meaning:** very large in amount, size, or importance

**Examples:**
1. The client represents a substantial share of our annual revenue.
2. There's a substantial opportunity in the small-business segment we haven't tapped.

---

### 1261. enrich → bolster

**Meaning:** to strengthen or improve something further

**Examples:**
1. The new hire's industry expertise will bolster our sales strategy.
2. Positive press coverage bolstered investor confidence ahead of the IPO.

---

### 1262. entangle → embroil

**Meaning:** to involve someone deeply and unwillingly in a conflict

**Examples:**
1. The company got embroiled in a lengthy trademark dispute with a competitor.
2. We don't want to embroil the sales team in the ongoing billing disagreement.

---

### 1263. enthusiastic → zealous

**Meaning:** showing great energy and passionate commitment

**Examples:**
1. Her zealous advocacy for customer feedback shaped the product roadmap.
2. The new marketing hire brings a zealous, results-driven approach to campaigns.

---

### 1264. entice → allure

**Meaning:** to attract someone with something appealing

**Examples:**
1. A generous signing bonus helped allure top candidates from competitors.
2. The limited-time offer is designed to allure lapsed subscribers back.

---

### 1265. entire → comprehensive

**Meaning:** covering all parts of something completely

**Examples:**
1. The consultant delivered a comprehensive review of our operations.
2. We need a comprehensive plan that covers every phase of the rollout.

---

### 1266. entitle → warrant

**Meaning:** to give someone a right or justify a particular action

**Examples:**
1. Premium members are warranted priority access to customer support.
2. The scale of the issue warrants an immediate response from leadership.

---

### 1267. entrust → commission

**Meaning:** to formally assign a task or responsibility to someone

**Examples:**
1. The board commissioned an external firm to audit our data practices.
2. We commissioned a market research team to validate the new pricing model.

---

### 1268. envy → covet

**Meaning:** to strongly wish you had something someone else has

**Examples:**
1. Competitors covet our customer retention rate, which is the best in the industry.
2. Smaller firms covet the brand recognition our company has built over decades.

---

### 1269. epic → monumental

**Meaning:** extremely large or significant in scale or impact

**Examples:**
1. Migrating the entire database was a monumental undertaking for the team.
2. Closing this account would be a monumental win for the sales division.

---

### 1270. erode → corrode

**Meaning:** to wear away or gradually weaken something

**Examples:**
1. Repeated missed deadlines began to corrode the client's trust in us.
2. Poor communication can corrode morale even on a high-performing team.

---

### 1271. erratic → volatile

**Meaning:** unpredictable and prone to sudden change

**Examples:**
1. The commodity's volatile pricing makes budgeting difficult for procurement.
2. Demand has been volatile since the supply chain disruptions began.

---

### 1272. escalate → intensify

**Meaning:** to become more serious or forceful

**Examples:**
1. Competition in this segment has intensified over the last two years.
2. Please intensify outreach efforts before the campaign deadline closes.

---

### 1273. escape → avert

**Meaning:** to prevent something bad from happening

**Examples:**
1. Quick action from the operations team helped avert a major service outage.
2. The revised contract terms averted a costly legal dispute with the supplier.

---

### 1274. esteem → prestige

**Meaning:** high status and respect earned through achievement or reputation

**Examples:**
1. Winning the industry award added considerable prestige to our brand.
2. The role carries a lot of prestige, but it also comes with high expectations.

---

### 1275. eternal → perpetual

**Meaning:** continuing forever or without any planned end

**Examples:**
1. We're stuck in a perpetual cycle of firefighting instead of planning ahead.
2. The license grants perpetual access to the software once purchased.

---

### 1276. evacuate → vacate

**Meaning:** to leave a place, often because it is required

**Examples:**
1. The team was asked to vacate the office floor during the renovation.
2. We must vacate the leased warehouse by the end of the contract term.

---

### 1277. evaporate → subside

**Meaning:** to gradually decrease and eventually disappear

**Examples:**
1. Investor concerns subsided once the quarterly numbers were released.
2. The initial backlash against the price change subsided within a week.

---

### 1278. exempt → immune

**Meaning:** not affected by or subject to something

**Examples:**
1. No department is immune to the budget cuts announced this quarter.
2. Long-term contracts are largely immune to short-term price fluctuations.

---

### 1279. evident → manifest

**Meaning:** clearly visible or obvious to anyone observing

**Examples:**
1. The benefits of the new process became manifest within the first month.
2. Customer dissatisfaction was manifest in the sharp drop in renewal rates.

---

### 1280. exaggerate → embellish

**Meaning:** to make something seem better or bigger than it really is

**Examples:**
1. Don't embellish the results in the report; stick to the verified numbers.
2. The vendor embellished the product's capabilities during the sales pitch.

---

### 1281. fail → falter

**Meaning:** to lose strength or stop working effectively

**Examples:**
1. Sales began to falter after the competitor entered the market.
2. The project faltered once two key engineers left the team.

---

### 1282. fade → recede

**Meaning:** to gradually become weaker or move further into the background

**Examples:**
1. Interest in the beta program receded once the novelty wore off.
2. As the crisis receded, leadership shifted focus back to growth initiatives.

---

### 1283. faint → tenuous

**Meaning:** weak or easily broken, lacking a strong basis

**Examples:**
1. The connection between the two metrics is tenuous at best.
2. We have only a tenuous lead over our closest competitor this quarter.

---

### 1284. false → erroneous

**Meaning:** incorrect because of a mistake

**Examples:**
1. The forecast was based on erroneous assumptions about customer demand.
2. We issued a correction after publishing erroneous figures in the report.

---

### 1285. familiar → acquainted

**Meaning:** having existing knowledge or experience of something

**Examples:**
1. New hires should become acquainted with our compliance policies in week one.
2. The consultant is well acquainted with regulations in this industry.

---

### 1286. famous → renowned

**Meaning:** widely known and admired for a particular quality

**Examples:**
1. The firm is renowned for its rigorous due diligence process.
2. We partnered with a renowned designer to refresh the brand identity.

---

### 1287. fancy → elaborate

**Meaning:** complex and detailed, often more than necessary

**Examples:**
1. The proposal doesn't need an elaborate design, just a clear message.
2. They built an elaborate loyalty program that few customers actually use.

---

### 1288. fantastic → exceptional

**Meaning:** unusually good or outstanding

**Examples:**
1. The team delivered exceptional results despite a tight deadline.
2. Her exceptional performance earned her a spot on the leadership track.

---

### 1289. fascinate → captivate

**Meaning:** to hold someone's attention completely through interest

**Examples:**
1. The demo captivated the entire room of prospective investors.
2. A compelling case study can captivate an audience far better than raw data.

---

### 1290. fashionable → contemporary

**Meaning:** reflecting current trends and styles

**Examples:**
1. The office redesign gives the space a more contemporary feel.
2. Our brand needed a contemporary refresh to appeal to younger customers.

---

### 1291. fasten → secure

**Meaning:** to attach or fix something firmly in place

**Examples:**
1. Make sure to secure the vendor's signature before releasing payment.
2. We've secured a long-term supply agreement with our preferred manufacturer.

---

### 1292. fat → generous

**Meaning:** larger or more than usual, often favorable

**Examples:**
1. This deal comes with a generous margin compared to our other accounts.
2. The client offered a generous budget for the rebranding project.

---

### 1293. fatal → catastrophic

**Meaning:** extremely damaging, often beyond recovery

**Examples:**
1. Ignoring the warning signs proved catastrophic for the product launch.
2. A single catastrophic failure in the payment system could cost us key clients.

---

### 1294. fatigue → exhaustion

**Meaning:** extreme tiredness that reduces effectiveness

**Examples:**
1. Meeting exhaustion is a real concern after back-to-back planning sessions.
2. The support team is showing signs of exhaustion after the busy launch week.

---

### 1295. fussy → meticulous

**Meaning:** paying very careful attention to small details

**Examples:**
1. The auditor was meticulous in checking every line of the expense report.
2. Our QA lead is meticulous about testing edge cases before release.

---

### 1296. fuzzy → ambiguous

**Meaning:** unclear or open to more than one interpretation

**Examples:**
1. The scope of work in the contract is too ambiguous to enforce.
2. Feedback from the pilot group was ambiguous about which feature they preferred.

---

### 1297. formidable → daunting

**Meaning:** intimidating because of its size, difficulty, or strength

**Examples:**
1. Entering the European market presents a daunting regulatory challenge.
2. The incumbent's market share makes it a daunting competitor to displace.

---

### 1298. feature → highlight

**Meaning:** to give special attention or prominence to something

**Examples:**
1. The keynote will highlight our new sustainability initiatives.
2. We should highlight the cost savings in the executive summary.

---

### 1299. feeble → ineffectual

**Meaning:** not producing the intended effect or result

**Examples:**
1. The initial response to the complaint was ineffectual and only escalated it further.
2. Our previous retention strategy proved ineffectual against the churn we're seeing now.

---

### 1300. feisty → assertive

**Meaning:** confident and direct in expressing opinions or demands

**Examples:**
1. She took an assertive stance during negotiations and secured better terms.
2. The candidate's assertive communication style impressed the hiring panel.

---

### 1301. fellow → counterpart

**Meaning:** a person holding a similar position in another team or organization

**Examples:**
1. Our finance lead met with her counterpart at the acquiring company.
2. I'll loop in my counterpart on the vendor's side to align on timelines.

---

### 1302. ferocious → aggressive

**Meaning:** forceful and determined, especially in competition

**Examples:**
1. We're facing aggressive pricing tactics from a new market entrant.
2. The firm set an aggressive growth target for the coming fiscal year.

---

### 1303. fertile → conducive

**Meaning:** providing favorable conditions for something to happen

**Examples:**
1. The open-office layout is more conducive to cross-team collaboration.
2. A flexible policy creates conditions conducive to innovation.

---

### 1304. fetch → retrieve

**Meaning:** to go and bring something back

**Examples:**
1. The system retrieves customer records automatically when a ticket is opened.
2. Please retrieve the signed contract from the archive before the meeting.

---

### 1305. feud → altercation

**Meaning:** a heated disagreement or dispute between parties

**Examples:**
1. HR had to mediate an altercation between two department heads.
2. The public altercation between the co-founders damaged investor confidence.

---

### 1306. fever → frenzy

**Meaning:** a state of wild, excited activity

**Examples:**
1. The limited-time offer triggered a buying frenzy on launch day.
2. A media frenzy followed the surprise announcement of the merger.

---

### 1307. fickle → capricious

**Meaning:** changing unpredictably, especially in preferences

**Examples:**
1. Consumer tastes in this category are notoriously capricious.
2. A capricious client can make long-term planning extremely difficult.

---

### 1308. field → domain

**Meaning:** an area of activity, knowledge, or responsibility

**Examples:**
1. Her expertise lies in the domain of cybersecurity compliance.
2. We're expanding into a new domain with the launch of our fintech product.

---

### 1309. fierce → intense

**Meaning:** very strong or extreme in force or degree

**Examples:**
1. Competition for talent in this sector has become extremely intense.
2. The negotiation grew intense as both sides pushed for better terms.

---

### 1310. fiery → passionate

**Meaning:** showing strong feeling and enthusiasm

**Examples:**
1. His passionate pitch convinced the panel to fund the project.
2. The founder gave a passionate speech about the company's mission.

---

### 1311. finite → constrained

**Meaning:** limited in amount or scope

**Examples:**
1. We're working with a constrained budget for this initiative.
2. The team has a constrained timeline to deliver the prototype.

---

### 1312. fishy → dubious

**Meaning:** seeming untrustworthy or suspicious

**Examples:**
1. The auditor flagged several dubious transactions in the ledger.
2. Investors grew wary of the company's dubious accounting practices.

---

### 1313. flag → spotlight

**Meaning:** to draw attention to something important

**Examples:**
1. The report spotlights three risks that need immediate attention.
2. Please spotlight any compliance gaps you find during the review.

---

### 1314. flair → panache

**Meaning:** a stylish and confident way of doing something

**Examples:**
1. She presents quarterly results with a panache that keeps the board engaged.
2. The team handled the crisis with unexpected panache, reassuring clients throughout.

---

### 1315. flare → erupt

**Meaning:** to suddenly break out or become intense

**Examples:**
1. Tensions erupted during the budget review meeting.
2. Complaints erupted on social media within hours of the price hike.

---

### 1316. flashy → ostentatious

**Meaning:** designed to attract attention, often excessively

**Examples:**
1. The client felt the proposed branding was too ostentatious for their audience.
2. We toned down the ostentatious graphics for a more professional look.

---

### 1317. forsake → relinquish

**Meaning:** to give up or let go of something, often reluctantly

**Examples:**
1. The founder had to relinquish majority control to secure the investment.
2. We won't relinquish our quality standards just to hit a shipping deadline.

---

### 1318. fleeting → transient

**Meaning:** lasting only a short time

**Examples:**
1. The spike in demand proved transient once the promotion ended.
2. Market advantages from a single feature are often transient at best.

---

### 1319. flimsy → insubstantial

**Meaning:** lacking strength or substance, easily challenged

**Examples:**
1. The legal team called the vendor's defense insubstantial and easily disproven.
2. Investors dismissed the projections as insubstantial without supporting data.

---

### 1320. flip → reverse

**Meaning:** to change something to the opposite direction or decision

**Examples:**
1. The committee reversed its earlier decision after reviewing new data.
2. We need to reverse the declining trend in customer satisfaction scores.

---

### 1321. float → posit

**Meaning:** to suggest an idea for consideration

**Examples:**
1. The strategy team posited a new pricing model during the offsite.
2. I'd like to posit an alternative approach before we finalize the plan.

---

### 1322. flock → converge

**Meaning:** to come together from different directions toward one point

**Examples:**
1. Attendees converged on the expo hall as soon as the doors opened.
2. All departments converged on a single shared roadmap this quarter.

---

### 1323. flop → flounder

**Meaning:** to struggle and fail to make progress

**Examples:**
1. The product floundered in the market after a weak launch campaign.
2. Without clear direction, the initiative floundered for months.

---

### 1324. flourish → thrive

**Meaning:** to grow or develop very successfully

**Examples:**
1. Small businesses have thrived under the new local tax incentives.
2. The team thrives when given clear goals and the autonomy to reach them.

---

### 1325. fluent → proficient

**Meaning:** highly skilled and capable in a particular area

**Examples:**
1. We need a candidate proficient in both data analysis and client communication.
2. Her team became proficient with the new platform within two weeks.

---

### 1326. fluid → adaptable

**Meaning:** able to change easily to suit new conditions

**Examples:**
1. Our roadmap stays adaptable so we can respond quickly to market shifts.
2. The remote work policy is adaptable to each team's specific needs.

---

### 1327. fluke → anomaly

**Meaning:** an unusual result that differs from the normal pattern

**Examples:**
1. The spike in signups turned out to be a one-time anomaly, not a trend.
2. Analysts flagged the quarter's numbers as an anomaly worth investigating further.

---

### 1328. flush → replete

**Meaning:** filled completely with something, often abundantly

**Examples:**
1. The startup is replete with cash after closing its Series B round.
2. This quarter's pipeline is replete with high-value enterprise leads.

---

### 1329. fluster → unnerve

**Meaning:** to make someone feel nervous or lose composure

**Examples:**
1. The unexpected question from the board unnerved the presenter.
2. Sudden changes to the agenda tend to unnerve less experienced staff.

---

### 1330. fold → collapse

**Meaning:** to fail completely and stop operating

**Examples:**
1. Two competitors collapsed after failing to secure additional funding.
2. The partnership nearly collapsed over disagreements on revenue sharing.

---

## Section: G – K (More)

### 1331. gadget → apparatus

**Meaning:** a piece of equipment or device, especially one with a specific technical function

**Examples:**
1. The new apparatus streamlines our inventory scanning process.
2. Engineering unveiled a compact apparatus that monitors machine performance in real time.

---

### 1332. gamble → venture

**Meaning:** to take a risky action in hopes of a favorable outcome

**Examples:**
1. The board chose to venture into the emerging markets despite the volatility.
2. Launching the product before testing was complete, the team decided to venture ahead regardless.

---

### 1333. general → overarching

**Meaning:** covering or including everything; all-encompassing

**Examples:**
1. The CEO outlined an overarching strategy for the next five years.
2. We need an overarching framework that aligns every department's goals.

---

### 1334. giant → behemoth

**Meaning:** an extremely large and powerful organization

**Examples:**
1. The startup now competes directly with industry behemoths.
2. Our client is a retail behemoth with operations in forty countries.

---

### 1335. gift → aptitude

**Meaning:** a natural ability or talent for something

**Examples:**
1. She has a genuine aptitude for negotiation.
2. The recruiter looks for candidates with an aptitude for data analysis.

---

### 1336. glance → peruse

**Meaning:** to look over or read something, often briefly

**Examples:**
1. Please peruse the attached contract before our call tomorrow.
2. The manager perused the quarterly figures during the commute.

---

### 1337. glitzy → ostentatious

**Meaning:** showy and extravagant in a way meant to impress

**Examples:**
1. The client complained the proposal felt ostentatious rather than substantive.
2. They toned down the ostentatious branding for the more conservative sector.

---

### 1338. glue → cohere

**Meaning:** to stick together or form a unified whole

**Examples:**
1. A shared mission helps the newly merged teams cohere quickly.
2. The presentation didn't cohere; the slides felt disconnected.

---

### 1339. goof → blunder

**Meaning:** to make a careless or embarrassing mistake

**Examples:**
1. The intern blundered by sending the draft to the entire client list.
2. We can't afford to blunder on a launch this high-profile.

---

### 1340. grade → appraise

**Meaning:** to assess the value or quality of something

**Examples:**
1. Managers appraise employee performance twice a year.
2. An independent consultant was brought in to appraise the merger proposal.

---

### 1341. grand → monumental

**Meaning:** extremely large or significant in scale or importance

**Examples:**
1. The CEO announced a monumental expansion into Southeast Asia.
2. Completing the merger was a monumental achievement for the finance team.

---

### 1342. grasp → comprehend

**Meaning:** to fully understand something

**Examples:**
1. New hires often take a few weeks to comprehend the full workflow.
2. It's essential that stakeholders comprehend the risks before signing off.

---

### 1343. greedy → avaricious

**Meaning:** excessively eager to acquire wealth or profit

**Examples:**
1. Critics accused the firm of avaricious pricing during the shortage.
2. An avaricious approach to cost-cutting eroded employee morale.

---

### 1344. green → unseasoned

**Meaning:** lacking experience or maturity in a role

1. The nonprofit partnered with several affiliated research institutions.
2. Employees at affiliated companies share access to the training portal.

---

### 1469. knuckle under → acquiesce

**Meaning:** to accept something reluctantly without protest

**Examples:**
1. The union eventually acquiesced to the revised contract terms.
2. Management acquiesced to the client's last-minute change request.

---

### 1470. kid stuff → rudimentary

**Meaning:** basic or elementary, lacking sophistication

**Examples:**
1. The onboarding module covers only rudimentary features of the platform.
2. Competitors quickly outpaced the company's rudimentary analytics tools.

---

### 1471. killjoy → naysayer

**Meaning:** a person who habitually expresses doubt or opposition

**Examples:**
1. Every team has a naysayer who questions each new initiative.
2. Despite the naysayers, leadership pushed forward with the pilot program.

---

### 1472. kid gloves → discretion

**Meaning:** careful and tactful handling of a sensitive matter

**Examples:**
1. HR handled the complaint with considerable discretion.
2. The account manager treated the volatile client relationship with discretion.

---

### 1473. knock-on effect → repercussion

**Meaning:** a consequence, often indirect, resulting from an earlier action

**Examples:**
1. The plant closure had repercussions across the entire supply chain.
2. Leadership weighed the repercussions before announcing the price change.

---

### 1474. kingdom → purview

**Meaning:** the range of a person's responsibility, knowledge, or authority

**Examples:**
1. Vendor negotiations fall outside my purview.
2. Data governance now sits within the CTO's purview.

---

### 1475. keel → equilibrium

**Meaning:** a state of balance and stability

**Examples:**
1. The new CFO helped restore financial equilibrium after a turbulent year.
2. The company worked hard to maintain equilibrium between growth and profitability.

---

### 1476. know-nothing → neophyte

**Meaning:** a person who is new to a subject or activity

**Examples:**
1. As a neophyte in the industry, she relied heavily on her mentor's guidance.
2. The training program is designed for neophytes with no prior coding experience.

---

### 1477. kick the habit → relinquish

**Meaning:** to voluntarily give up or stop doing something

**Examples:**
1. The company relinquished its reliance on a single supplier after the shortage.
2. Leadership relinquished the outdated approval process in favor of automation.

---

### 1478. kindling → catalyst

**Meaning:** something that causes or accelerates significant change

**Examples:**
1. The customer complaint became the catalyst for a full product redesign.
2. Falling costs served as a catalyst for the industry's rapid growth.

---

### 1479. kick into gear → galvanize

**Meaning:** to spur someone into action, often suddenly

**Examples:**
1. The looming deadline galvanized the entire team.
2. The CEO's speech galvanized employees to embrace the new strategy.

---

### 1480. kayo → vanquish

**Meaning:** to completely defeat a rival or competitor

**Examples:**
1. The company's new pricing strategy vanquished its closest competitor within a year.
2. A superior product roadmap helped the startup vanquish more established rivals.

---

## Section: L – O (More)

### 1481. label → designate

**Meaning:** to formally assign or identify something as a particular category

**Examples:**
1. The finance team designated Q3 as a transition period for the new accounting system.
2. Management designated three regional offices as pilot sites for the new policy.

---

### 1482. labor → exertion

**Meaning:** the effort or hard work put into a task

**Examples:**
1. The project required significant exertion from the entire engineering team over the weekend.
2. Her exertion during the negotiation paid off with a favorable contract.

---

### 1483. land → clinch

**Meaning:** to successfully secure a deal, client, or agreement

**Examples:**
1. The sales team clinched a major contract with the retail chain last week.
2. After months of negotiation, we finally clinched the partnership deal.

---

### 1484. language → terminology

**Meaning:** the specific words and expressions used within a field or profession

**Examples:**
1. New hires must familiarize themselves with the company's internal terminology.
2. The contract's terminology was reviewed by legal before signing.

---

### 1485. lapse → hiatus

**Meaning:** a temporary pause or gap in activity

**Examples:**
1. Production experienced a brief hiatus while the supplier resolved shipping delays.
2. The marketing campaign resumed after a two-week hiatus.

---

### 1486. lawsuit → litigation

**Meaning:** a legal case brought before a court

**Examples:**
1. The company set aside a reserve fund to cover potential litigation costs.
2. Both parties agreed to settle rather than proceed with litigation.

---

### 1487. layoff → workforce reduction

**Meaning:** the elimination of jobs, usually to cut costs

**Examples:**
1. The board approved a workforce reduction of ten percent to control expenses.
2. Employees were notified of the upcoming workforce reduction during the town hall.

---

### 1488. lazy → indolent

**Meaning:** unwilling to work or make an effort

**Examples:**
1. The manager flagged the indolent approach to deadlines as a performance concern.
2. An indolent attitude toward client follow-ups cost the team a key account.

---

### 1489. leak → divulge

**Meaning:** to reveal confidential information, intentionally or not

**Examples:**
1. An employee accidentally divulged the merger details before the official announcement.
2. The spokesperson was careful not to divulge unreleased financial figures.

---

### 1490. lean → streamlined

**Meaning:** efficient, with unnecessary elements removed

**Examples:**
1. The new workflow is far more streamlined than the previous manual process.
2. We adopted a streamlined approval chain to speed up procurement.

---

### 1491. leap → surge

**Meaning:** a sudden, significant increase

**Examples:**
1. The company saw a surge in demand following the product launch.
2. Stock prices experienced a surge after the earnings report.

---

### 1492. legit → legitimate

**Meaning:** genuine, valid, and lawful

**Examples:**
1. The auditor confirmed that all expense claims were legitimate.
2. Investors wanted proof that the business opportunity was legitimate.

---

### 1493. lend → extend

**Meaning:** to provide, especially credit or support

**Examples:**
1. The bank agreed to extend a line of credit to the growing startup.
2. Senior leadership extended their support to the new initiative.

---

### 1494. lengthen → prolong

**Meaning:** to make something last longer

**Examples:**
1. Supply chain issues prolonged the delivery timeline by several weeks.
2. The dispute prolonged negotiations well past the original deadline.

---

### 1495. lengthy → protracted

**Meaning:** unusually long, especially in duration

**Examples:**
1. The merger underwent a protracted review by regulators.
2. After protracted discussions, the two firms reached an agreement.

---

### 1496. lenient → permissive

**Meaning:** allowing considerable freedom, not strict

**Examples:**
1. The company's permissive remote-work policy improved employee satisfaction.
2. A permissive expense policy led to unexpected budget overruns.

---

### 1497. lessen → mitigate

**Meaning:** to reduce the severity or impact of something

**Examples:**
1. The team implemented safeguards to mitigate project risk.
2. Diversifying suppliers helped mitigate the impact of the shortage.

---

### 1498. letdown → anticlimax

**Meaning:** a disappointing outcome after high expectations

**Examples:**
1. The product launch was an anticlimax compared to the pre-release hype.
2. Investors viewed the quarterly results as an anticlimax after the bullish forecast.

---

### 1499. liberal → magnanimous

**Meaning:** generous in giving or acting

**Examples:**
1. The CEO was magnanimous in crediting the entire team for the successful launch.
2. The client offered a magnanimous extension on the payment deadline.

---

### 1500. license → accreditation

**Meaning:** official recognition or certification to operate or practice

**Examples:**
1. The firm obtained accreditation to handle international shipments.
2. Consultants must maintain accreditation to advise on regulatory matters.

---

### 1501. lifelong → perpetual

**Meaning:** continuing indefinitely, without end

**Examples:**
1. The vendor agreement includes a perpetual license for the software.
2. The foundation was set up to provide perpetual funding for research.

---

### 1502. lifespan → longevity

**Meaning:** the length of time something lasts or remains useful

**Examples:**
1. The engineering team tested the product's longevity under heavy use.
2. Client longevity is a key metric for measuring relationship success.

---

### 1503. lifetime → tenure

**Meaning:** the period during which someone holds a position or role

**Examples:**
1. During his tenure as CFO, the company doubled its revenue.
2. Her tenure on the board brought valuable industry expertise.

---

### 1504. likable → personable

**Meaning:** having a pleasant, friendly manner that others respond well to

**Examples:**
1. The candidate's personable style impressed the interview panel.
2. A personable account manager builds stronger client relationships.

---

### 1505. likelihood → propensity

**Meaning:** the tendency or chance that something will happen

**Examples:**
1. The audit assessed the company's propensity for compliance risk.
2. Repeat customers show a higher propensity to upgrade their plans.

---

### 1506. likewise → correspondingly

**Meaning:** in a similar or matching way

**Examples:**
1. Revenue grew ten percent; expenses rose correspondingly.
2. Demand increased in Q2, and correspondingly, production was ramped up.

---

### 1507. limitless → boundless

**Meaning:** without limits, extremely great

**Examples:**
1. The startup's founders approached the market with boundless enthusiasm.
2. The new platform offers boundless scalability for growing enterprises.

---

### 1508. limp → sluggish

**Meaning:** lacking energy or moving slowly

**Examples:**
1. Sales remained sluggish throughout the first quarter.
2. A sluggish economy dampened consumer spending across the sector.

---

### 1509. linger → persist

**Meaning:** to continue to exist for longer than expected

**Examples:**
1. Concerns about supply reliability persisted despite the new contract.
2. Low morale persisted after the reorganization announcement.

---

### 1510. liquidate → divest

**Meaning:** to sell off assets or a business unit

**Examples:**
1. The conglomerate decided to divest its underperforming division.
2. Shareholders pushed the board to divest non-core assets.

---

### 1511. listless → lethargic

**Meaning:** lacking energy or enthusiasm

**Examples:**
1. The team seemed lethargic after weeks of back-to-back deadlines.
2. A lethargic response from customer support frustrated key clients.

---

### 1512. little → trivial

**Meaning:** of very small importance or value

**Examples:**
1. The committee dismissed the objection as trivial and moved forward.
2. What seemed like a trivial detail ended up delaying the launch.

---

### 1513. loan → financing

**Meaning:** money provided, usually to be repaid, to fund an activity

**Examples:**
1. The company secured financing to expand its manufacturing capacity.
2. Investors provided bridge financing while the funding round closed.

---

### 1514. lobby → advocate

**Meaning:** to actively support or argue in favor of something

**Examples:**
1. The trade association advocated for lighter regulation on small businesses.
2. The department head advocated for additional headcount during budget season.

---

### 1515. locate → pinpoint

**Meaning:** to identify the exact position or source of something

**Examples:**
1. The analytics team pinpointed the source of the revenue decline.
2. Auditors pinpointed several gaps in the internal control process.

---

### 1516. lock in → secure

**Meaning:** to obtain and guarantee something firmly

**Examples:**
1. The vendor secured a three-year exclusive supply agreement.
2. We secured favorable pricing before the tariff changes took effect.

---

### 1517. lodge → file

**Meaning:** to formally submit a complaint, claim, or document

**Examples:**
1. The customer filed a formal complaint regarding the delayed shipment.
2. Legal filed the necessary paperwork ahead of the regulatory deadline.

---

### 1518. lofty → aspirational

**Meaning:** ambitious, reflecting high hopes or goals

**Examples:**
1. The five-year plan set aspirational revenue targets for the region.
2. Leadership presented an aspirational vision for the company's future.

---

### 1519. logic → rationale

**Meaning:** the underlying reasoning behind a decision

**Examples:**
1. The board requested a clear rationale for the restructuring proposal.
2. The presentation laid out the rationale behind the pricing strategy.

---

### 1520. lone → sole

**Meaning:** being the only one

**Examples:**
1. The startup was the sole bidder for the government contract.
2. She was the sole dissenting voice in the board meeting.

---

### 1521. lonely → isolated

**Meaning:** separated from others, lacking connection

**Examples:**
1. Remote employees reported feeling isolated from the core team.
2. The regional office operated in isolated fashion from headquarters.

---

### 1522. lousy → substandard

**Meaning:** of poor quality, below an acceptable standard

**Examples:**
1. The client complained about the substandard service received last quarter.
2. Substandard materials led to a costly product recall.

---

### 1523. loom → impend

**Meaning:** to be about to happen, especially something significant

**Examples:**
1. A tighter budget impends as the fiscal year draws to a close.
2. With the deadline impending, the team worked through the weekend.

---

### 1524. lure → entice

**Meaning:** to attract someone with an appealing offer

**Examples:**
1. The company enticed top talent with a competitive benefits package.
2. A limited-time discount enticed new customers to sign up.

---

### 1525. lopsided → disproportionate

**Meaning:** unbalanced, with parts unequal in size or weight

**Examples:**
1. The budget allocation was disproportionate, favoring marketing over R&D.
2. Analysts noted a disproportionate reliance on a single client account.

---

### 1526. maintain → sustain

**Meaning:** to keep something going at the same level over time

**Examples:**
1. The company worked hard to sustain its market leadership position.
2. Consistent quality helped sustain customer loyalty through the downturn.

---

### 1527. maintenance → upkeep

**Meaning:** the work needed to keep something in good condition

**Examples:**
1. The facilities budget covers routine upkeep of office equipment.
2. Regular upkeep of the software platform prevents costly outages.

---

### 1528. mandate → stipulate

**Meaning:** to specify as a requirement

**Examples:**
1. The contract stipulates a thirty-day notice period for cancellation.
2. Compliance policy stipulates annual training for all staff.

---

### 1529. mandatory → obligatory

**Meaning:** required, not optional

**Examples:**
1. Attendance at the compliance briefing is obligatory for all managers.
2. Submitting expense receipts is obligatory under the new finance policy.

---

### 1530. maneuver → tactic

**Meaning:** a planned action used to achieve a specific goal

**Examples:**
1. The pricing tactic helped the company win market share quickly.
2. Competitors questioned the tactic behind the sudden acquisition.

---

### 1531. manifest → materialize

**Meaning:** to become real or apparent

**Examples:**
1. The projected cost savings failed to materialize as expected.
2. Early warning signs materialized into a full supply chain crisis.

---

### 1532. manipulate → finesse

**Meaning:** to handle a situation skillfully, sometimes cleverly

**Examples:**
1. The negotiator finessed the contract terms to satisfy both parties.
2. She finessed the tense client call into a productive discussion.

---

### 1533. manpower → workforce

**Meaning:** the number of people available to do work

**Examples:**
1. The project was delayed due to insufficient workforce capacity.
2. The plant expanded its workforce ahead of the holiday season.

---

### 1534. mar → tarnish

**Meaning:** to damage the quality or reputation of something

**Examples:**
1. The data breach tarnished the company's reputation with customers.
2. A single product recall can tarnish years of brand trust.

---

### 1535. margin → differential

**Meaning:** the gap or difference between two values, such as cost and price

**Examples:**
1. The pricing differential between the two product lines narrowed this year.
2. Currency fluctuations affected the profit differential across regions.

---

### 1536. mask → disguise

**Meaning:** to hide or conceal the true nature of something

**Examples:**
1. The one-time gain disguised weaker underlying operating performance.
2. Aggressive discounting disguised a decline in genuine demand.

---

### 1537. mass → aggregate

**Meaning:** a total combined amount or collection

**Examples:**
1. The aggregate revenue across all divisions exceeded projections.
2. Analysts reviewed the aggregate data before issuing the forecast.

---

### 1538. master → command

**Meaning:** to gain a thorough and confident understanding of something

**Examples:**
1. New analysts are expected to command the financial modeling tools within a month.
2. She commands a deep understanding of the regulatory landscape.

---

### 1539. masterful → adept

**Meaning:** highly skilled at doing something

**Examples:**
1. The CFO was adept at navigating complex tax regulations.
2. Her adept handling of the crisis reassured investors.

---

### 1540. material → substantive

**Meaning:** significant and meaningful, not superficial

**Examples:**
1. The board requested substantive changes to the risk management plan.
2. The report offered substantive evidence supporting the expansion.

---

### 1541. maximize → optimize

**Meaning:** to make the best or most effective use of something

**Examples:**
1. The team optimized the supply chain to reduce shipping costs.
2. Marketing optimized ad spend to improve conversion rates.

---

### 1542. meager → paltry

**Meaning:** very small in amount, insufficient

**Examples:**
1. Shareholders were disappointed by the paltry dividend increase.
2. The department operated on a paltry budget compared to competitors.

---

### 1543. meantime → interim

**Meaning:** the period between two events

**Examples:**
1. An interim CEO was appointed while the board conducted its search.
2. In the interim, the team relied on the legacy system.

---

### 1544. measly → scant

**Meaning:** barely sufficient, very small

**Examples:**
1. The proposal received scant support during the review meeting.
2. Scant evidence was presented to justify the budget increase.

---

### 1545. mechanism → apparatus

**Meaning:** a system or set of processes used to achieve something

**Examples:**
1. The company built a robust apparatus for tracking compliance issues.
2. The new feedback apparatus helps managers act on employee concerns.

---

### 1546. media → press

**Meaning:** news organizations and journalists collectively

**Examples:**
1. The press covered the product launch extensively.
2. The company issued a statement to the press following the incident.

---

### 1547. mediate → arbitrate

**Meaning:** to help resolve a dispute between two parties

**Examples:**
1. An external consultant was brought in to arbitrate the contract dispute.
2. HR arbitrated the disagreement between the two department heads.

---

### 1548. medium → intermediate

**Meaning:** falling between two levels, neither high nor low

**Examples:**

2. The company set an intermediate revenue target before the final goal.

---

### 1549. mellow → subdued

**Meaning:** calm, restrained, lacking intensity

**Examples:**
1. Trading remained subdued ahead of the earnings announcement.
2. The mood at the meeting was subdued following the layoffs.

---

### 1550. memo → communiqué

**Meaning:** an official written message or notice

**Examples:**
1. The executive team issued a communiqué outlining the new strategy.
2. A brief communiqué informed employees of the policy update.

---

### 1551. memorable → indelible

**Meaning:** leaving a lasting impression

**Examples:**
1. The keynote speech left an indelible impression on attendees.
2. The brand made an indelible mark on the industry with its rebrand.

---

### 1552. mentor → groom

**Meaning:** to train and prepare someone for a future role

**Examples:**
1. Senior partners groom junior associates for leadership positions.
2. The company grooms high performers for management roles early on.

---

### 1553. merit → warrant

**Meaning:** to deserve or justify something

**Examples:**
1. The strong results warrant an increase in next year's budget.
2. The complaint warrants a full internal investigation.

---

### 1554. mess → disarray

**Meaning:** a state of confusion or disorder

**Examples:**
1. The transition left the inventory system in disarray.
2. Poor planning threw the project schedule into disarray.

---

### 1555. messy → chaotic

**Meaning:** disorganized and confusing

**Examples:**
1. The onboarding process was chaotic during the system migration.
2. A chaotic rollout led to numerous customer complaints.

---

### 1556. midpoint → juncture

**Meaning:** a particular point or stage in a process

**Examples:**

2. The project reached a critical juncture after the first milestone.

---

### 1557. migrate → transition

**Meaning:** to move from one system, place, or state to another

**Examples:**
1. The IT department transitioned all users to the new platform.
2. The company is transitioning to a fully digital invoicing process.

---

### 1558. milestone → benchmark

**Meaning:** a standard or point used to measure progress

**Examples:**
1. The team set clear benchmarks to track project progress.
2. Quarterly benchmarks help leadership evaluate performance.

---

### 1559. mimic → emulate

**Meaning:** to copy or imitate the behavior of something

2. The training program emulates real client scenarios.

---

**Meaning:** to pay careful attention to something

**Examples:**

---

### 1561. mindful → cognizant

**Meaning:** fully aware of a fact or situation

**Examples:**

2. Employees should stay cognizant of the updated compliance guidelines.

### 1562. mindset → paradigm

**Meaning:** a particular way of thinking or set of assumptions

**Examples:**
1. The company adopted a customer-first paradigm across all departments.
2. Shifting to a data-driven paradigm improved decision-making.

---

### 1563. market → commercialize

**Meaning:** to develop and sell a product for profit

**Examples:**

2. It took two years to commercialize the research into a viable product.

### 1564. marketplace → arena

**Examples:**

### 1565. mock → simulate

### 1566. naive → credulous

### 1567. narrative → account

### 1568. nascent → emerging

### 1577. nerve → audacity

### 1578. net → yield

### 1581. nitpick → quibble

### 1582. noble → laudable

### 1584. noisy → boisterous

### 1585. nominal → token

### 1587. nonchalant → blasé

2. The team's blasé response to the complaint frustrated the client.

### 1588. nondescript → innocuous

### 1591. nosedive → plummet

### 1592. notch → echelon

### 1593. notion → premise

### 1595. notorious → infamous
