### 1596. obey → comply

**Meaning:** to act in accordance with a rule or instruction

**Examples:**
1. All departments must comply with the updated data privacy regulations.
2. The vendor failed to comply with the agreed delivery schedule.

---

### 1597. object → dissent

**Meaning:** to formally disagree or express opposition

**Examples:**
1. One board member dissented from the majority decision on the merger.
2. Employees dissented openly during the town hall discussion.

---

### 1598. objection → reservation

**Meaning:** a feeling of doubt or hesitation about a plan

**Examples:**
1. The legal team raised reservations about the proposed contract terms.
2. Investors expressed reservations about the aggressive growth targets.

---

### 1599. oblige → compel

**Meaning:** to force or require someone to do something

**Examples:**
1. New regulations compel companies to disclose emissions data annually.
2. The contract compels both parties to resolve disputes through arbitration.

---

### 1600. obliterate → eradicate

**Meaning:** to completely eliminate something

**Examples:**
1. The new process eradicated most manual data entry errors.
2. Automation helped eradicate the backlog within weeks.

---

### 1601. oblivious → unwitting

**Meaning:** unaware of something, without realizing it

**Examples:**
1. The firm was an unwitting participant in the supplier's fraud scheme.
2. Employees remained unwitting of the upcoming restructuring until the announcement.

---

### 1602. obscure → conceal

**Meaning:** to hide or make something difficult to see or understand

**Examples:**
1. The footnotes concealed the true extent of the company's liabilities.
2. Vague language in the report concealed the declining margins.

---

### 1603. observe → discern

**Meaning:** to notice or detect something through careful attention

**Examples:**
1. Analysts discerned a shift in consumer purchasing patterns.
2. The auditor discerned inconsistencies in the quarterly filings.

---

### 1604. observation → appraisal

**Meaning:** a judgment or assessment based on careful examination

**Examples:**
1. The manager's appraisal of the team's performance was largely positive.
2. The consultant's appraisal highlighted several operational gaps.

---

### 1605. obsessed → preoccupied

**Meaning:** thinking about something constantly, to the exclusion of other things

**Examples:**
1. The team was preoccupied with the product launch and missed other deadlines.
2. Leadership grew preoccupied with short-term results at the expense of strategy.

---

### 1606. obstacle → impediment

**Meaning:** something that blocks progress

**Examples:**
1. Regulatory approval remains the biggest impediment to the merger.
2. Outdated technology was a major impediment to scaling operations.

---

### 1607. obstruct → hinder

**Meaning:** to create difficulty for or slow down progress

**Examples:**
1. Bureaucratic delays hindered the rollout of the new system.
2. Poor communication hindered collaboration between departments.

---

### 1608. obtain → procure

**Meaning:** to acquire or get something, often through effort

**Examples:**
1. The procurement team worked to procure raw materials at a lower cost.
2. The company procured the necessary licenses before entering the market.

---

### 1609. occupy → assume

**Meaning:** to take on a role or position

**Examples:**
1. She will assume the role of regional director starting next month.
2. The interim leader assumed responsibility for the division during the transition.

---

### 1610. occur → transpire

**Meaning:** to happen or take place

**Examples:**
1. Several delays transpired during the system migration.
2. It later transpired that the vendor had missed several key deadlines.

---

### 1611. occurrence → incidence

**Meaning:** the rate or frequency at which something happens

**Examples:**
1. The incidence of customer complaints dropped after the process change.
2. A rising incidence of returns prompted a review of the product line.

---

### 1612. offend → antagonize

**Meaning:** to cause anger or resentment through one's actions

**Examples:**
1. The abrupt policy change antagonized several long-standing clients.
2. Aggressive sales tactics can antagonize potential partners.

---

### 1613. offense → transgression

**Meaning:** a violation of a rule or standard

**Examples:**
1. The compliance breach was treated as a serious transgression.
2. Repeated transgressions of the code of conduct led to termination.

---

### 1614. offhand → impromptu

**Meaning:** done without preparation, spontaneous

**Examples:**
1. The CEO gave an impromptu response to the reporter's question.
2. An impromptu meeting was called to address the outage.

---

### 1615. official → sanctioned

**Meaning:** formally approved or authorized

**Examples:**
1. The new remote-work policy is now officially sanctioned by HR.
2. Only sanctioned vendors may be used for procurement.

---

### 1616. offload → transfer

**Meaning:** to pass on a task, responsibility, or asset to someone else

**Examples:**
1. The manager transferred several low-priority tasks to a junior team member.
2. The company transferred the underperforming asset to a new owner.

---

### 1617. offset → counterbalance

**Meaning:** to reduce or neutralize the effect of something with an opposing factor

**Examples:**
1. Cost savings in logistics helped counterbalance rising raw material prices.
2. The strong overseas performance counterbalanced weak domestic sales.

---

### 1618. offshoot → spinoff

**Meaning:** a separate entity that develops from an existing organization

**Examples:**
1. The startup began as a spinoff from the university's research lab.
2. The new division operates as an independent spinoff of the parent company.

---

### 1619. omission → exclusion

**Meaning:** something left out or not included

**Examples:**
1. The exclusion of key data skewed the initial forecast.
2. Auditors flagged the exclusion of several transactions from the report.

---

### 1620. omit → bypass

**Meaning:** to leave out or skip over

**Examples:**
1. The team bypassed the standard review step to meet the deadline.
2. Bypassing the approval process led to a compliance violation.

---

### 1621. onboard → induct

**Meaning:** to formally introduce and integrate a new employee

**Examples:**
1. HR inducted twelve new hires into the company this month.
2. The mentorship program helps induct graduates into their roles quickly.

---

### 1622. onslaught → barrage

**Meaning:** a sudden, intense wave of something

**Examples:**
1. The support team faced a barrage of complaints after the outage.
2. A barrage of negative reviews followed the price increase.

---

### 1623. opaque → impenetrable

**Meaning:** difficult to understand or see through

**Examples:**
1. The pricing structure was so impenetrable that customers frequently called support.
2. Investors criticized the impenetrable language in the annual report.

---

### 1624. operate → function

**Meaning:** to work or perform in a particular way

**Examples:**
1. The new system functions independently of the legacy platform.
2. The regional office functions under a decentralized management model.

---

### 1625. operation → undertaking

**Meaning:** a task or project, especially a significant one

**Examples:**
1. The system migration was a massive undertaking involving three departments.
2. Launching in a new market proved to be a costly undertaking.

---

### 1626. opponent → adversary

**Meaning:** a rival or competitor

**Examples:**
1. The firm's biggest adversary announced a similar product last week.
2. Negotiators treated the supplier as an adversary rather than a partner.

---

### 1627. opportunistic → expedient

**Meaning:** acting for immediate advantage, sometimes at the expense of principle

**Examples:**
1. The acquisition was seen as an expedient move to eliminate a competitor.
2. Critics called the pricing change an expedient response to falling profits.

---

### 1628. opportunity → prospect

**Meaning:** a possibility for future success or gain

**Examples:**
1. The new market represents a strong prospect for revenue growth.
2. Sales identified several promising prospects at the trade show.

---

### 1629. oppose → contest

**Meaning:** to formally challenge or disagree with a decision

**Examples:**
1. The union contested the proposed changes to overtime pay.
2. Shareholders contested the board's decision to reject the buyout offer.

---

### 1630. organize → orchestrate

**Meaning:** to carefully plan and coordinate a complex event or effort

**Examples:**
1. The events team orchestrated a flawless product launch.
2. Leadership orchestrated the restructuring to minimize disruption.

---

## Section: P – R (More)

### 1631. pack → consolidate

**Meaning:** to combine multiple things into a single, more compact whole

**Examples:**
1. We need to consolidate the three regional reports into one master file before the board meeting.
2. The merger will consolidate the two companies' supply chains into a single operation.

---

### 1632. panel → consortium

**Meaning:** a group of organizations or experts working together on a shared goal

**Examples:**
1. A consortium of five universities is co-developing the new AI ethics curriculum.
2. Our proposal was reviewed by a consortium of industry advisors before approval.

---

### 1633. panic → consternation

**Meaning:** a feeling of anxiety or dismay caused by something unexpected

**Examples:**
1. The sudden stock drop caused consternation among the board members.
2. News of the delayed launch was met with consternation from the sales team.

---

### 1634. paper → dossier

**Meaning:** a detailed collection of documents on a particular subject or person

**Examples:**
1. HR compiled a full dossier on the candidate before the final interview round.
2. The legal team assembled a dossier of evidence to support the compliance claim.

---

### 1635. parcel → consignment

**Meaning:** a batch of goods shipped or delivered together

**Examples:**
1. The consignment of samples arrived a day late, delaying the product demo.
2. We tracked the consignment through customs to confirm the delivery window.

---

### 1636. pardon → exonerate

**Meaning:** to officially clear someone of blame or wrongdoing

**Examples:**
1. The internal audit exonerated the finance team of any wrongdoing.
2. The investigation exonerated the vendor once the invoice discrepancy was explained.

---

### 1637. park → shelve

**Meaning:** to set an idea or plan aside for later consideration

**Examples:**
1. Given the budget cuts, we've decided to shelve the office redesign project for now.
2. Leadership shelved the acquisition talks until market conditions improve.

---

### 1638. party → entity

**Meaning:** a distinct organization or individual involved in a business matter

**Examples:**
1. All entities involved in the contract must sign before the deal closes.
2. The third-party entity handling logistics has its own compliance requirements.

---

### 1639. parrot → echo

**Meaning:** to repeat or reflect someone else's words or sentiment

**Examples:**
1. The regional managers echoed the CEO's concerns about rising costs.
2. Customer feedback echoed the same complaint about slow onboarding.

---

### 1640. patch → remediate

**Meaning:** to fix or correct a problem, especially a temporary one

**Examples:**
1. IT is working to remediate the security vulnerability before the audit.
2. We remediated the data error within hours of it being flagged.

---

### 1641. pause → suspend

**Meaning:** to temporarily stop an activity or process

**Examples:**
1. The company suspended all hiring until the next fiscal quarter.
2. We had to suspend the campaign after the platform outage.

---

### 1642. pilfer → misappropriate

**Meaning:** to wrongfully take or use something, especially funds, for oneself

**Examples:**
1. The auditor discovered that a former employee had misappropriated petty cash funds.
2. The company faced legal action after allegedly misappropriating client deposits.

---

### 1643. peek → preview

**Meaning:** to see or show something briefly before its official release

**Examples:**
1. We gave key clients an early preview of the new dashboard feature.
2. The marketing team previewed the campaign visuals during the planning call.

---

### 1644. peer → counterpart

**Meaning:** a person holding a similar position in another organization or team

**Examples:**
1. Our CFO met with her counterpart at the acquiring firm to align on financials.
2. I reached out to my counterpart in the London office for regional insights.

---

### 1645. pen → compose

**Meaning:** to carefully write or draft something, such as a message or document

**Examples:**
1. She composed a thoughtful response to the client's escalation email.
2. The communications team composed the press release ahead of the announcement.

---

### 1646. pending → outstanding

**Meaning:** not yet resolved, paid, or completed

**Examples:**
1. There are three outstanding invoices that need to be cleared before month-end.
2. We still have several outstanding action items from last week's meeting.

---

### 1647. perceive → discern

**Meaning:** to notice or understand something, often something subtle

**Examples:**
1. Analysts discerned a shift in consumer sentiment from the latest survey data.
2. It was hard to discern the real cause of the delay from the vague update.

---

### 1648. perk → incentive

**Meaning:** a benefit offered to encourage a certain behavior or attract talent

**Examples:**
1. The company added a wellness stipend as a new employee incentive.
2. Sales reps receive a quarterly incentive tied to their performance targets.

---

### 1649. permission → authorization

**Meaning:** official approval to do something

**Examples:**
1. You'll need authorization from finance before booking any travel over budget.
2. The vendor cannot access the system without proper authorization.

---

### 1650. perplex → confound

**Meaning:** to confuse or baffle someone

**Examples:**
1. The conflicting sales figures confounded the entire analytics team.
2. Investors were confounded by the sudden change in guidance.

---

### 1651. pool → cohort

**Meaning:** a group of people who share a common characteristic, such as a hiring or training group

**Examples:**
1. This year's graduate cohort will complete a six-week rotation program.
2. We're analyzing retention rates across different customer cohorts.

---

### 1652. petition → lobby

**Meaning:** to try to persuade decision-makers to support a particular cause

**Examples:**
1. The department is lobbying leadership for additional headcount next quarter.
2. Industry groups lobbied regulators to delay the new compliance deadline.

---

### 1653. phone → liaise

**Meaning:** to communicate and cooperate with someone, usually to coordinate work

**Examples:**
1. Please liaise with the vendor to confirm the delivery schedule.
2. Our regional lead will liaise with the design team on the rebrand.

---

### 1654. picture → panorama

**Meaning:** a broad, comprehensive view of a situation

**Examples:**
1. The quarterly review gave the board a clearer panorama of company performance.
2. This dashboard offers a panorama of activity across all business units.

---

### 1655. prop up → bolster

**Meaning:** to strengthen or support something

**Examples:**
1. The new marketing push helped bolster sagging quarterly sales.
2. Additional funding was used to bolster the struggling product line.

---

### 1656. peruse → appraise

**Meaning:** to examine and judge the value or quality of something

**Examples:**
1. The committee appraised each proposal before shortlisting finalists.
2. An external consultant was brought in to appraise the company's assets.

---

### 1657. pin down → ascertain

**Meaning:** to find out or determine something with certainty

**Examples:**
1. We need to ascertain the root cause before rolling out a permanent fix.
2. The auditors ascertained that the discrepancy was a clerical error.

---

### 1658. pinpoint → isolate

**Meaning:** to identify something precisely, separating it from other factors

**Examples:**
1. The engineering team isolated the bug within a single line of code.
2. We isolated the market segment driving most of our recent growth.

---

### 1659. pit against → juxtapose

**Meaning:** to place two things side by side for comparison

**Examples:**
1. The report juxtaposes this quarter's earnings with last year's for context.
2. The presentation juxtaposed our pricing model against three competitors'.

---

### 1660. pity → commiserate

**Meaning:** to express sympathy for someone's difficult situation

**Examples:**
1. Colleagues commiserated with the sales team after they missed the target.
2. The manager commiserated with staff over the delayed bonus payouts.

---

### 1661. plate → bandwidth

**Meaning:** the mental or time capacity available to take on more work

**Examples:**
1. I don't have the bandwidth to take on another client this month.
2. The team's bandwidth is stretched thin with the year-end audit underway.

---

### 1662. prune → streamline

**Meaning:** to make a process more efficient by removing unnecessary elements

**Examples:**
1. We streamlined the approval workflow to cut turnaround time in half.
2. The new software helped streamline our inventory tracking process.

---

### 1663. puff up → embellish

**Meaning:** to make something seem more impressive than it really is

**Examples:**
1. The candidate appeared to embellish his achievements on his resume.
2. Don't embellish the results in the report; stick to the actual figures.

---

### 1664. pledge → underwrite

**Meaning:** to formally commit to financially supporting something

**Examples:**
1. The bank agreed to underwrite the company's expansion loan.
2. A major sponsor underwrote the entire conference budget this year.

---

### 1665. plentiful → abundant

**Meaning:** existing in large quantities

**Examples:**
1. Talent in this field is abundant right now, making recruitment easier.
2. There is abundant evidence that remote work boosts productivity for our teams.

---

### 1666. plug → endorse

**Meaning:** to publicly support or recommend a product or idea

**Examples:**
1. The influencer agreed to endorse our new product line on social media.
2. Several industry leaders have endorsed the proposed safety standards.

---

### 1667. plunge → plummet

**Meaning:** to drop suddenly and sharply

**Examples:**
1. Shares plummeted after the disappointing earnings call.
2. Customer satisfaction scores plummeted following the outage.

---

### 1668. plus → asset

**Meaning:** a useful or valuable quality, person, or resource

**Examples:**
1. Her bilingual skills are a real asset to the international sales team.
2. The company's brand reputation remains its greatest asset.

---

### 1669. pocket → appropriate

**Meaning:** to take something, often money, for one's own use

**Examples:**
1. An employee was found to have appropriated funds from the events budget.
2. The committee appropriated part of the surplus for training programs.

---

### 1670. poison → taint

**Meaning:** to spoil or damage something's reputation or quality

**Examples:**
1. The scandal tainted the brand's image for years afterward.
2. A single bad review can taint an otherwise strong product launch.

---

### 1671. poke around → probe

**Meaning:** to investigate something carefully to find information

**Examples:**
1. Regulators are probing the company's accounting practices.
2. The journalist probed deeper into the firm's supply chain sourcing.

---

### 1672. polish → refine

**Meaning:** to improve something by making small, careful adjustments

**Examples:**
1. We spent an extra week refining the pitch deck before the investor meeting.
2. The team refined the onboarding flow based on user feedback.

---

### 1673. ponder → deliberate

**Meaning:** to think carefully about something before deciding

**Examples:**
1. The board deliberated for hours before approving the merger.
2. Take some time to deliberate on the offer before responding.

---

### 1674. pop up → emerge

**Meaning:** to appear or come into existence, often unexpectedly

**Examples:**
1. A new competitor emerged in the market just weeks after our launch.
2. Several concerns emerged during the risk assessment meeting.

---

### 1675. portray → depict

**Meaning:** to represent or show something in a particular way

**Examples:**
1. The annual report depicts steady growth across all divisions.
2. The case study depicts how the client overcame their supply issues.

---

### 1676. pose → constitute

**Meaning:** to represent or amount to something, such as a risk or issue

**Examples:**
1. The outdated software constitutes a significant security risk.
2. Late payments constitute a breach of the service agreement.

---

### 1677. post → circulate

**Meaning:** to distribute information to a wider group of people

**Examples:**
1. Please circulate the meeting notes to everyone on the project team.
2. The updated policy will be circulated to all departments by Friday.

---

### 1678. postpone → defer

**Meaning:** to put something off until a later time

**Examples:**
1. We've decided to defer the product launch to next quarter.
2. The committee deferred its decision pending further market research.

---

### 1679. pour → channel

**Meaning:** to direct resources or effort toward a specific purpose

**Examples:**
1. The company is channeling most of its budget into R&D this year.
2. We channeled our energy into fixing the customer complaint backlog.

---

### 1680. poverty → scarcity

**Meaning:** a shortage or insufficient supply of something valuable

**Examples:**
1. Talent scarcity in the tech sector has driven up recruitment costs.
2. Resource scarcity forced the team to prioritize only critical projects.

---

### 1681. preach → advocate

**Meaning:** to publicly support or argue in favor of an idea

**Examples:**
1. The CFO has long advocated for more conservative spending practices.
2. Employees are encouraged to advocate for sustainable business practices.

---

### 1682. precede → predate

**Meaning:** to exist or happen before something else in time

**Examples:**
1. This partnership predates the company's rebranding by several years.
2. The original contract predates the current leadership team.

---

### 1683. precious → invaluable

**Meaning:** extremely useful or valuable

**Examples:**
1. Her institutional knowledge has proven invaluable during the transition.
2. The client feedback was invaluable in shaping the next product update.

---

### 1684. purge → excise

**Meaning:** to remove something completely, often unwanted material

**Examples:**
1. We excised the outdated clauses from the vendor contract.
2. The editor excised redundant sections from the annual report.

---

### 1685. pare down → condense

**Meaning:** to make something shorter or more compact

**Examples:**
1. Please condense the 40-slide deck into a 10-slide executive summary.
2. We condensed the onboarding process from five days to two.

---

### 1686. pitch → proposition

**Meaning:** a formal offer or suggestion, often presented for approval

**Examples:**
1. The startup presented a compelling proposition to the venture capital firm.
2. Our sales proposition emphasizes long-term cost savings over price.

---

### 1687. prod → galvanize

**Meaning:** to encourage or push someone into action

**Examples:**
1. The keynote speech galvanized the sales team ahead of the new quarter.
2. Falling market share galvanized leadership into launching a turnaround plan.

---

### 1688. prone → susceptible

**Meaning:** likely to be affected by something, often negatively

**Examples:**
1. Manual data entry is especially susceptible to human error.
2. Smaller vendors are more susceptible to supply chain disruptions.

---

### 1689. props → accolades

**Meaning:** praise or awards given in recognition of achievement

**Examples:**
1. The design team received accolades from clients for the rebrand.
2. Her leadership during the crisis earned accolades across the industry.

---

### 1690. pump → infuse

**Meaning:** to introduce something, such as money or energy, into a system

**Examples:**
1. Investors infused fresh capital into the company after the funding round.
2. The new hire infused the team with much-needed creative energy.

---

### 1691. quiz → debrief

**Meaning:** to question someone in detail, often after an event

**Examples:**
1. Management debriefed the sales team after the conference to gather insights.
2. We were debriefed on the client meeting as soon as we got back to the office.

---

### 1692. quirk → idiosyncrasy

**Meaning:** an unusual or distinctive feature or habit

**Examples:**
1. Every legacy system has its own idiosyncrasies that new staff must learn.
2. The client's approval process has a few idiosyncrasies worth noting.

---

### 1693. quarrel → dispute

**Meaning:** a disagreement or argument between parties

**Examples:**
1. The two departments had a dispute over budget allocation.
2. A contractual dispute delayed the project by several weeks.

---

### 1694. qualify → satisfy

**Meaning:** to meet the necessary requirements or conditions

**Examples:**
1. The vendor must satisfy all compliance criteria before onboarding.
2. This candidate satisfies every requirement for the senior role.

---

### 1695. query → inquiry

**Meaning:** a formal request for information

**Examples:**
1. We received an inquiry from a journalist about the recent layoffs.
2. Please direct all pricing inquiries to the sales department.

---

### 1696. quaint → antiquated

**Meaning:** old-fashioned and no longer suited to modern needs

**Examples:**
1. Our filing system feels antiquated compared to competitors' digital tools.
2. The company finally retired its antiquated approval process.

---

### 1697. queue → backlog

**Meaning:** a buildup of tasks or work that has not yet been completed

**Examples:**
1. Support tickets have piled into a significant backlog since the outage.
2. We're clearing the project backlog before starting new initiatives.

---

### 1698. quell → mitigate

**Meaning:** to reduce the severity or impact of something

**Examples:**
1. The press release was issued to mitigate investor concerns.
2. New safety protocols were introduced to mitigate workplace risks.

---

### 1699. quench → satiate

**Meaning:** to fully satisfy a need or desire

**Examples:**
1. The new feature set finally satiated customer demand for customization.
2. No single hire can satiate the team's need for specialized skills.

---

### 1700. quandary → conundrum

**Meaning:** a difficult problem with no easy solution

**Examples:**
1. Leadership faced a conundrum over whether to cut costs or invest further.
2. The pricing conundrum required input from both finance and marketing.

---

### 1701. qualm → reservation

**Meaning:** a feeling of doubt or hesitation about something

**Examples:**
1. Several board members expressed reservations about the acquisition.
2. I have a few reservations about the timeline we've committed to.

---

### 1702. quash → nullify

**Meaning:** to make something invalid or ineffective

**Examples:**
1. The updated agreement nullifies all previous verbal commitments.
2. Legal moved quickly to nullify the fraudulent contract.

---

### 1703. quibble → contest

**Meaning:** to formally disagree with or challenge something

**Examples:**
1. The vendor contested the penalty clause in the final invoice.
2. We contested the auditor's findings and requested a re-review.

---

### 1704. quota → threshold

**Meaning:** a minimum level or limit that must be reached

**Examples:**
1. Reps must hit the sales threshold to qualify for the bonus tier.
2. Once orders cross the threshold, the discount is applied automatically.

---

### 1705. remake → reinvent

**Meaning:** to change something so significantly that it becomes essentially new

**Examples:**
1. The company reinvented its entire product line after the market shift.
2. We need to reinvent our customer service model for the digital age.

---

### 1706. remark → opine

**Meaning:** to express a formal opinion

**Examples:**
1. The analyst opined that the sector was heading for a correction.
2. Several panelists opined on the future of remote work during the summit.

---

### 1707. remember → recall

**Meaning:** to bring a piece of information back to mind

**Examples:**
1. Please recall the key points from last quarter's strategy session.
2. The client couldn't recall the exact terms discussed on the call.

---

### 1708. remind → reiterate

**Meaning:** to say something again for emphasis or clarity

**Examples:**
1. I want to reiterate that the deadline cannot be extended.
2. The manager reiterated the importance of following the new protocol.

---

### 1709. remove → expunge

**Meaning:** to completely erase or delete something from a record

**Examples:**
1. The error was expunged from the client's account history.
2. We expunged the outdated clauses from the employee handbook.

---

### 1710. rent → lease

**Meaning:** a formal agreement to use property or equipment for payment

**Examples:**
1. The company decided to lease office space rather than purchase it.
2. We lease our fleet vehicles on a three-year rolling contract.

---

### 1711. repair → refurbish

**Meaning:** to restore something to good condition, often improving it

**Examples:**
1. The office was refurbished before the new hires started.
2. We refurbished the old equipment instead of buying new units.

---

### 1712. repeat → replicate

**Meaning:** to reproduce or copy something exactly

**Examples:**
1. We plan to replicate this campaign's success in other markets.
2. The lab was unable to replicate the initial test results.

---

### 1713. replace → substitute

**Meaning:** to put something in place of another thing

**Examples:**
1. We substituted the original vendor after repeated delivery delays.
2. A digital form was substituted for the old paper process.

---

### 1714. reply → correspond

**Meaning:** to communicate with someone, especially in writing

**Examples:**
1. Please correspond directly with the client regarding scheduling changes.
2. The legal teams corresponded for weeks before finalizing the terms.

---

### 1715. report → brief

**Meaning:** to give someone a concise summary of information

**Examples:**
1. The project lead briefed executives on the rollout progress.
2. We were briefed on the merger details ahead of the public announcement.

---

### 1716. represent → epitomize

**Meaning:** to be a perfect example of something

**Examples:**
1. This campaign epitomizes everything our brand stands for.
2. Her leadership style epitomizes the company's collaborative culture.

---

### 1717. request → solicit

**Meaning:** to formally ask for something, such as feedback or funding

**Examples:**
1. We solicited feedback from customers before finalizing the redesign.
2. The startup solicited investment from several venture capital firms.

---

### 1718. require → necessitate

**Meaning:** to make something necessary

**Examples:**
1. The new regulations necessitate a full review of our data policies.
2. Rapid growth necessitated hiring an additional finance manager.

---

### 1719. rescue → salvage

**Meaning:** to save something from failure or loss

**Examples:**
1. The team worked overtime to salvage the client relationship after the error.
2. We managed to salvage most of the budget despite the project delays.

---

### 1720. research → investigate

**Meaning:** to examine a subject systematically to discover facts

**Examples:**
1. The compliance team is investigating the source of the data breach.
2. We investigated three vendors before selecting our new supplier.

---

### 1721. resemble → mirror

**Meaning:** to closely match or reflect something else

**Examples:**
1. Our growth pattern mirrors what we saw in the previous fiscal year.
2. The new policy mirrors best practices adopted by industry leaders.

---

### 1722. reserve → earmark

**Meaning:** to set aside for a specific purpose

**Examples:**
1. Funds have been earmarked for the upcoming office expansion.
2. We earmarked a portion of the budget for employee training.

---

### 1723. reset → recalibrate

**Meaning:** to adjust or realign something to a new standard

**Examples:**
1. The team recalibrated its targets after the mid-year review.
2. We need to recalibrate our forecast given the recent market shift.

---

### 1724. resign → relinquish

**Meaning:** to formally give up a position or responsibility

**Examples:**
1. She relinquished her role as team lead to focus on strategy.
2. The founder relinquished day-to-day control after the acquisition.

---

### 1725. resist → withstand

**Meaning:** to remain strong or unaffected despite pressure

**Examples:**
1. The business model withstood the economic downturn better than expected.
2. Our supply chain withstood the disruption thanks to diversified sourcing.

---

### 1726. resolve → reconcile

**Meaning:** to settle differences and bring things into agreement

**Examples:**
1. Finance and operations reconciled their conflicting budget figures.
2. The mediator helped reconcile the dispute between the two partners.

---

### 1727. resource → capital

**Meaning:** valuable assets, especially money or people, used to generate value

**Examples:**
1. Human capital remains our most important resource for growth.
2. The firm raised additional capital to fund its expansion plans.

---

### 1728. respect → esteem

**Meaning:** great admiration or regard for someone

**Examples:**
1. The team holds their mentor in high esteem for her guidance.
2. Clients esteem the firm for its consistent, honest communication.

---

### 1729. respond → reciprocate

**Meaning:** to respond to an action with a similar action

**Examples:**
1. The vendor reciprocated our flexibility by extending the payment terms.
2. We reciprocated the client's goodwill with a discounted renewal offer.

---

### 1730. rest → residual

**Meaning:** what remains after the main part has been accounted for

**Examples:**
1. The residual budget will be allocated to team training next quarter.
2. After covering fixed costs, the residual profit was reinvested.

---

### 1731. restore → reinstate

**Meaning:** to bring something back to its previous state or position

**Examples:**
1. The company reinstated the employee discount program after feedback.
2. Access to the system was reinstated once the security review cleared.

---

### 1732. restrict → curtail

**Meaning:** to limit or reduce something

**Examples:**
1. Travel budgets were curtailed for the remainder of the fiscal year.
2. The new policy curtails access to sensitive client data.

---

### 1733. result → ramification

**Meaning:** a consequence, often significant, of an action or decision

**Examples:**
1. The ramifications of the delayed launch are still being assessed.
2. Leadership considered the long-term ramifications before approving the layoffs.

---

### 1734. resume → recommence

**Meaning:** to start again after a pause

**Examples:**
1. Production will recommence once the equipment repairs are complete.
2. Negotiations recommenced after both sides agreed to new terms.

---

### 1735. retain → sustain

**Meaning:** to keep something going over time

**Examples:**
1. The company worked hard to sustain its market share despite new competitors.
2. We introduced new benefits to sustain employee morale.

---

### 1736. retire → decommission

**Meaning:** to officially take something out of use

**Examples:**
1. The legacy software will be decommissioned by the end of the year.
2. We decommissioned the old server after migrating to the cloud.

---

### 1737. retreat → withdraw

**Meaning:** to pull back from a position or commitment

**Examples:**
1. The company withdrew from the international market after weak sales.
2. Investors withdrew their support once the risks became clear.

---

### 1738. retrieve → recoup

**Meaning:** to get back a loss, especially of money

**Examples:**
1. The firm managed to recoup its losses within two quarters.
2. We're hoping to recoup the marketing spend through increased sales.

---

### 1739. return → revert

**Meaning:** to go back to a previous state or plan

**Examples:**
1. We decided to revert to the original pricing structure after customer pushback.
2. The team reverted to the earlier design once testing revealed issues.

---

### 1740. reveal → disclose

**Meaning:** to make previously hidden information known

**Examples:**
1. The company must disclose any material financial risks to shareholders.
2. We disclosed the data breach to affected customers within 48 hours.

---

### 1741. revenge → retaliation

**Meaning:** an action taken in response to a perceived wrong, often harmful

**Examples:**
1. Analysts warned that a price war could trigger retaliation from competitors.
2. The tariffs were seen as retaliation for the earlier trade restrictions.

---

### 1742. reverse → rescind

**Meaning:** to officially cancel or take back a decision

**Examples:**
1. The board rescinded its approval after new information came to light.
2. We had to rescind the job offer following a failed background check.

---

### 1743. review → scrutinize

**Meaning:** to examine something very closely and critically

**Examples:**
1. Auditors scrutinized every line item in the year-end report.
2. Investors are scrutinizing the company's spending ahead of the IPO.

---

### 1744. revise → amend

**Meaning:** to make changes to a document or agreement

**Examples:**
1. We amended the contract to reflect the new delivery timeline.
2. The policy was amended after employee feedback highlighted gaps.

---

### 1745. revive → revitalize

**Meaning:** to bring new energy or success to something

**Examples:**
1. The rebranding effort helped revitalize a struggling product line.
2. New leadership revitalized the team's morale within months.

---

### 1746. reward → incentivize

**Meaning:** to motivate someone with a benefit tied to performance

**Examples:**
1. The bonus structure was redesigned to incentivize long-term retention.
2. We incentivized early adoption of the new software with a small stipend.

---

### 1747. rich → lucrative

**Meaning:** producing a great deal of profit

**Examples:**
1. The Asian market has proven especially lucrative for our brand.
2. They signed a lucrative deal with a major distributor last quarter.

---

### 1748. rid → eliminate

**Meaning:** to completely remove or get rid of something

**Examples:**
1. The new workflow eliminates several redundant approval steps.
2. We eliminated three underperforming product lines this year.

---

### 1749. ride → capitalize

**Meaning:** to take advantage of an opportunity or trend

**Examples:**
1. The startup capitalized on the sudden surge in remote work demand.
2. We're capitalizing on the holiday season with a targeted promotion.

---

### 1750. ridicule → deride

**Meaning:** to mock or express contempt for something

**Examples:**
1. Critics derided the rebrand as a step backward for the company.
2. The proposal was derided by shareholders as overly risky.

---

### 1751. rift → schism

**Meaning:** a serious division or disagreement within a group

**Examples:**
1. A schism emerged between the founders over the company's direction.
2. The merger created a schism between the two corporate cultures.

---

### 1752. right → rectify

**Meaning:** to correct something that is wrong

**Examples:**
1. We're taking steps to rectify the billing error immediately.
2. The team worked overnight to rectify the shipping mistake.

---

### 1753. rigid → stringent

**Meaning:** strict and demanding exact compliance

**Examples:**
1. The client imposed stringent quality standards on every shipment.
2. New stringent regulations require additional compliance reporting.

---

### 1754. rise → escalate

**Meaning:** to increase quickly or become more intense

**Examples:**
1. Customer complaints escalated after the price change was announced.
2. Tensions between the two departments escalated over resource allocation.

---

### 1755. risk → exposure

**Meaning:** the extent to which something is vulnerable to loss or harm

**Examples:**
1. The company reduced its exposure by diversifying suppliers.
2. Currency fluctuations increased the firm's financial exposure abroad.

---

### 1756. rival → adversary

**Meaning:** a competitor or opponent in business

**Examples:**
1. Our chief adversary in this market just launched a similar product.
2. The firm outperformed its closest adversary in customer satisfaction ratings.

---

### 1757. road → trajectory

**Meaning:** the general direction or path something is heading

**Examples:**
1. The company's growth trajectory has impressed investors this year.
2. Leadership outlined a clear trajectory for the next five years.

---

### 1758. rob → plunder

**Meaning:** to take something valuable through unfair or aggressive means

**Examples:**
1. Critics accused the executives of plundering the pension fund.
2. The startup felt its ideas had been plundered by a larger competitor.

---

### 1759. rock → destabilize

**Meaning:** to make something less stable or secure

**Examples:**
1. The scandal threatened to destabilize investor confidence.
2. Sudden leadership changes can destabilize an otherwise strong team.

---

### 1760. role → capacity

**Meaning:** the specific function or position someone holds

**Examples:**
1. In his capacity as project lead, he approved the final budget.
2. She joined the board in an advisory capacity last spring.

---

### 1761. roll out → deploy

**Meaning:** to introduce or make something available for use

**Examples:**
1. The new CRM system will be deployed across all regional offices.
2. We deployed the updated policy company-wide last Monday.

---

### 1762. room → latitude

**Meaning:** freedom to act or make decisions within certain limits

**Examples:**
1. Managers were given more latitude to approve small expenses on their own.
2. The contract offers little latitude for renegotiating the price.

---

### 1763. root → genesis

**Meaning:** the origin or starting point of something

**Examples:**
1. The genesis of the product idea came from a customer support call.
2. Understanding the genesis of the complaint helped us fix the process.

---

### 1764. rough → approximate

**Meaning:** close to accurate but not exact

**Examples:**
1. Can you give me an approximate figure for next quarter's revenue?
2. We used an approximate timeline since the final scope wasn't set.

---

### 1765. round → aggregate

**Meaning:** to combine figures or items into a total

**Examples:**
1. The report aggregates sales data from all regional branches.
2. We aggregated customer feedback into a single sentiment score.

---

### 1766. route → divert

**Meaning:** to send something along a different path than originally planned

**Examples:**
1. Incoming support calls were diverted to the overflow team during the outage.
2. We diverted part of the marketing budget toward digital ads.

---

### 1767. routine → customary

**Meaning:** usual or standard practice

**Examples:**
1. It's customary for new hires to complete a two-week orientation.
2. The customary review process was skipped due to the tight deadline.

---

### 1768. rude → discourteous

**Meaning:** lacking good manners or respect toward others

**Examples:**
1. The client complained about a discourteous interaction with support staff.
2. Discourteous behavior from vendors was flagged in the supplier review.

---

### 1769. ruin → decimate

**Meaning:** to severely damage or destroy something

**Examples:**
1. The scandal decimated the brand's reputation within days.
2. Aggressive discounting decimated the profit margins across the category.

---

### 1770. rule → adjudicate

**Meaning:** to make an official decision or judgment on a dispute

**Examples:**
1. The arbitration panel adjudicated the contract dispute in our favor.
2. HR was asked to adjudicate the disagreement between the two managers.

---

### 1771. rumor → speculation

**Meaning:** information or theories that are unconfirmed

**Examples:**
1. There's growing speculation about a possible leadership change.
2. The company declined to comment on speculation about the merger.

---

### 1772. run → spearhead

**Meaning:** to lead an initiative or take charge of an effort

**Examples:**
1. She was chosen to spearhead the company's sustainability initiative.
2. He spearheaded the launch of the new customer portal.

---

### 1773. rush → expedite

**Meaning:** to make something happen more quickly

**Examples:**
1. We asked the supplier to expedite the shipment given the tight deadline.
2. The approval process was expedited for high-priority clients.

---

### 1774. rust → atrophy

**Meaning:** to gradually weaken or decline from lack of use

**Examples:**
1. Without regular practice, even strong presentation skills can atrophy.
2. The team's technical skills began to atrophy after the tool was retired.

---

### 1775. ruthless → draconian

**Meaning:** excessively harsh or severe

**Examples:**
1. Employees pushed back against the draconian new expense policy.
2. The draconian cost-cutting measures led to several key resignations.

---

### 1776. race → vie

**Meaning:** to compete eagerly for something

**Examples:**
1. Three vendors are vying for the exclusive supply contract.
2. Candidates are vying for the newly created director position.

---

### 1777. radical → transformative

**Meaning:** causing a major, fundamental change

**Examples:**
1. The new leadership introduced transformative changes to the company culture.
2. Cloud migration proved transformative for the firm's operations.

---

### 1778. raid → pillage

**Meaning:** to take resources aggressively and without regard for consequences

**Examples:**
1. Competitors were accused of pillaging our talent pool with aggressive offers.
2. The department felt its budget had been pillaged to fund other projects.

---

### 1779. ramp up → accelerate

**Meaning:** to increase the pace or speed of something

**Examples:**
1. We're accelerating hiring to meet the new project deadline.
2. The company accelerated production ahead of the holiday season.

---

### 1780. reaction → repercussion

**Meaning:** a consequence or effect resulting from an action

**Examples:**
1. The price increase had significant repercussions on customer loyalty.
2. Leadership weighed the potential repercussions before announcing the layoffs.

---

## Section: S – Z (More)

### 1781. sad → despondent

**Meaning:** feeling very low or hopeless about a situation

**Examples:**
1. The team grew despondent after losing the client to a competitor.
2. Morale was despondent following the second round of layoffs.

---

### 1782. sample → exemplar

**Meaning:** a model example that represents the best of its kind

**Examples:**
1. This proposal is an exemplar of how we should structure future pitches.
2. The new hire's onboarding plan is an exemplar for the whole department.

---

### 1783. satisfy → gratify

**Meaning:** to please or fulfill someone's expectations

**Examples:**
1. The vendor worked overtime to gratify the client's last-minute requests.
2. Delivering ahead of schedule gratified the entire steering committee.

---

### 1784. scarce → sparse

**Meaning:** existing in small amounts, not plentiful

**Examples:**
1. Engineering talent remains sparse in this region, driving up salaries.
2. Attendance at the optional workshop was sparse this quarter.

---

### 1785. scared → apprehensive

**Meaning:** nervous or worried about something that might happen

**Examples:**
1. Investors are apprehensive about entering the market amid rising rates.
2. The junior analyst felt apprehensive before presenting to the board.

---

### 1786. scatter → disperse

**Meaning:** to spread out over a wide area

**Examples:**
1. The reorganization will disperse the support team across three regional offices.
2. Attendees dispersed quickly once the keynote concluded.

---

### 1787. scheduled → slated

**Meaning:** planned or arranged to happen at a set time

**Examples:**
1. The product launch is slated for early October.
2. Two executives are slated to speak at next month's conference.

---

### 1788. scold → reprimand

**Meaning:** to formally criticize someone for a mistake

**Examples:**
1. HR issued a written reprimand after the compliance breach.
2. The manager chose to reprimand the vendor privately rather than in the group call.

---

### 1789. scope → ambit

**Meaning:** the range or extent of something's authority or influence

**Examples:**
1. Data privacy now falls within the ambit of the legal department.
2. This task sits outside the ambit of our current contract.

---

### 1790. scrap → rescind

**Meaning:** to officially cancel or withdraw a plan or decision

**Examples:**
1. The board voted to rescind the relocation plan after employee pushback.
2. Management rescinded the hiring freeze once revenue rebounded.

---

### 1791. screen → vet

**Meaning:** to carefully check or evaluate someone or something before approval

**Examples:**
1. All new suppliers must be vetted by procurement before onboarding.
2. The recruiter vets every candidate against the security clearance checklist.

---

### 1792. search → scour

**Meaning:** to search thoroughly and carefully

**Examples:**
1. We scoured the archives for the original contract terms.
2. The team scoured competitor filings to benchmark pricing strategy.

---

### 1793. secret → undisclosed

**Meaning:** not made known or revealed publicly

**Examples:**
1. The acquisition was made for an undisclosed sum.
2. Details of the settlement remain undisclosed pending legal review.

---

### 1794. section → segment

**Meaning:** a distinct part of a larger whole

**Examples:**
1. The premium segment now accounts for a third of total revenue.
2. Please review the compliance segment of the report before submission.

---

### 1795. seem → purport

**Meaning:** to claim or appear to be something, often with doubt implied

**Examples:**
1. The vendor purports to offer round-the-clock support, but reviews suggest otherwise.
2. The report purports to show a full recovery in customer satisfaction.

---

### 1796. select → curate

**Meaning:** to thoughtfully choose and assemble a set of items

**Examples:**
1. Marketing curated a shortlist of case studies for the investor deck.
2. The panel curated the finalists from over two hundred applicants.

---

### 1797. sensible → judicious

**Meaning:** showing good sense and sound judgment

**Examples:**
1. A judicious use of the reserve fund kept the project on track.
2. The CFO made a judicious call to delay the expansion by a quarter.

---

### 1798. sensitive → delicate

**Meaning:** requiring careful and tactful handling

**Examples:**
1. Negotiating the severance package is a delicate matter.
2. The merger talks are at a delicate stage and must stay confidential.

---

### 1799. separate → segregate

**Meaning:** to keep distinctly apart from something else

**Examples:**
1. Auditors require us to segregate client funds from operating capital.
2. The new policy segregates duties to reduce fraud risk.

---

### 1800. serious → grave

**Meaning:** very significant or worrying in nature

**Examples:**
1. The data breach poses a grave risk to customer trust.
2. Leadership treated the compliance lapse as a grave concern.

---

### 1801. serve → cater to

**Meaning:** to provide what is needed or wanted by a particular group

**Examples:**
1. Our new pricing tier caters to small and mid-sized businesses.
2. The platform was redesigned to cater to enterprise clients.

---

### 1802. service → provision

**Meaning:** the act of supplying or arranging something needed

**Examples:**
1. The contract covers the provision of ongoing technical support.
2. We are reviewing the provision of cloud infrastructure across regions.

---

### 1803. setback → impediment

**Meaning:** something that blocks or slows down progress

**Examples:**
1. Regulatory delays proved a major impediment to the rollout.
2. Staffing shortages remain the biggest impediment to hitting the deadline.

---

### 1804. several → numerous

**Meaning:** many, more than a few

**Examples:**
1. Numerous departments contributed feedback to the revised policy.
2. The vendor has numerous clients in the healthcare sector.

---

### 1805. severe → acute

**Meaning:** extremely serious or intense

**Examples:**
1. The company is facing an acute shortage of skilled engineers.
2. Cash flow pressures became acute during the slow season.

---

### 1806. shady → dubious

**Meaning:** questionable or of doubtful honesty

**Examples:**
1. The auditor flagged several dubious entries in the expense report.
2. We declined the partnership due to the vendor's dubious reputation.

---

### 1807. shake-up → overhaul

**Meaning:** a thorough and complete change or reorganization

**Examples:**
1. The new CEO announced a full overhaul of the leadership team.
2. An overhaul of the supply chain reduced delivery times by half.

---

### 1808. shallow → superficial

**Meaning:** lacking depth or thoroughness

**Examples:**
1. The initial market analysis was too superficial to guide investment.
2. A superficial review missed the underlying compliance issue.

---

### 1809. shape → formulate

**Meaning:** to carefully develop a plan or idea

**Examples:**
1. The strategy team will formulate a response to the new tariffs.
2. We need to formulate a clear message before the press briefing.

---

### 1810. shipment → consignment

**Meaning:** a batch of goods sent to a buyer

**Examples:**
1. The consignment cleared customs two days behind schedule.
2. Please confirm receipt of the latest consignment from our supplier.

---

### 1811. shortfall → deficit

**Meaning:** an amount by which something falls short of what is needed

**Examples:**
1. The budget shows a deficit of nearly two hundred thousand dollars.
2. We must close the talent deficit before the next expansion phase.

---

### 1812. shout → exclaim

**Meaning:** to speak suddenly and loudly out of strong emotion

**Examples:**
1. The founder exclaimed with delight when the funding round closed.
2. Investors exclaimed in surprise at the quarter's growth figures.

---

### 1813. sprout → burgeon

**Meaning:** to grow or develop quickly

**Examples:**
1. New satellite offices are burgeoning across the region.
2. A burgeoning demand for the app forced us to scale servers overnight.

---

### 1814. shut → seal

**Meaning:** to close something completely, often finalizing an agreement

**Examples:**
1. Both parties met to seal the partnership with a signed agreement.
2. We aim to seal the deal before the fiscal year ends.

---

### 1815. shy → reticent

**Meaning:** reluctant to speak openly or share information

**Examples:**
1. The vendor was reticent about disclosing their pricing model.
2. New hires are often reticent to challenge senior leadership in meetings.

---

### 1816. sick → indisposed

**Meaning:** temporarily unable to work due to illness

**Examples:**
1. The director was indisposed and missed the quarterly review.
2. Please reschedule the call; our contact is indisposed today.

---

### 1817. sign → execute

**Meaning:** to formally complete and put a document into effect

**Examples:**
1. Both parties will execute the agreement by end of week.
2. Legal is waiting for the client to execute the final contract.

---

### 1818. significant → substantial

**Meaning:** large or important enough to matter

**Examples:**
1. The merger brought substantial cost savings within the first year.
2. We saw a substantial rise in customer retention after the update.

---

### 1819. silly → frivolous

**Meaning:** not serious or sensible, wasting time or resources

**Examples:**
1. The committee rejected the request as a frivolous use of the budget.
2. Leadership dismissed the complaint as frivolous and moved on.

---

### 1820. sizeable → considerable

**Meaning:** fairly large in size, amount, or extent

**Examples:**
1. The firm has invested a considerable sum in employee training.
2. There is considerable interest in the new product line overseas.

---

### 1821. single → sole

**Meaning:** being the only one of its kind

**Examples:**
1. She is the sole shareholder with veto power over major decisions.
2. The vendor is our sole source for this critical component.

---

### 1822. size → magnitude

**Meaning:** the great scale or extent of something

**Examples:**
1. The magnitude of the data loss forced an immediate audit.
2. Leadership underestimated the magnitude of the integration challenge.

---

### 1823. sketch → delineate

**Meaning:** to describe or outline something precisely

**Examples:**
1. The proposal delineates each phase of the rollout timeline.
2. Please delineate the responsibilities of each team before kickoff.

---

### 1824. skill → proficiency

**Meaning:** a high level of competence in a particular area

**Examples:**
1. The role requires strong proficiency in financial modeling.
2. Her proficiency in negotiation closed the deal ahead of schedule.

---

### 1825. skilled → adept

**Meaning:** very good at doing something through practice

**Examples:**
1. He is adept at managing cross-functional teams under pressure.
2. The firm hired an analyst adept at forecasting market trends.

---

### 1826. skillful → deft

**Meaning:** showing great skill and quick, precise handling

**Examples:**
1. Her deft handling of the media crisis protected the brand's reputation.
2. The mediator's deft approach resolved the dispute in one session.

---

### 1827. slack → lax

**Meaning:** not strict enough, careless about standards

**Examples:**
1. The audit found lax controls around expense approvals.
2. Security protocols had grown lax after the merger.

---

### 1828. slam → denounce

**Meaning:** to publicly criticize something strongly

**Examples:**
1. Analysts denounced the pricing change as tone-deaf.
2. The union denounced the proposed cuts to overtime pay.

---

### 1829. sleepy → lethargic

**Meaning:** lacking energy and enthusiasm

**Examples:**
1. The afternoon session felt lethargic after the long lunch break.

---

### 1830. slice → sliver

**Meaning:** a very small portion of something

**Examples:**

---

### 1831. slight → marginal

**Meaning:** small in amount or degree, barely noticeable

**Examples:**
1. The update produced only a marginal improvement in load times.
2. Sales showed a marginal uptick despite the marketing push.

---

### 1832. slip → lapse

**Meaning:** a small mistake or failure to do something properly

**Examples:**
1. A lapse in the approval process let the invoice go unpaid.

---

### 1833. sloppy → slipshod

**Meaning:** careless and lacking attention to detail

**Examples:**

---

### 1834. soar → escalate

**Meaning:** to rise quickly and dramatically

**Examples:**

---

### 1835. snag → complication

1. A licensing complication pushed the launch back two weeks.

### 1836. sneaky → surreptitious

**Meaning:** done secretly to avoid being noticed

**Examples:**

2. Competitors took a surreptitious look at our pricing sheet during the demo.

---

### 1837. soft → lenient

**Meaning:** not strict, allowing more flexibility than usual

**Examples:**
1. The vendor offered lenient payment terms during the downturn.

---

### 1838. soften → mitigate

**Meaning:** to reduce the harmful effect of something

**Examples:**
1. The hedging strategy helped mitigate currency risk.
2. Additional training was introduced to mitigate the rise in errors.

---

### 1839. sore → contentious

**Meaning:** likely to cause disagreement or argument

**Examples:**

---

### 1840. sorry → contrite

**Meaning:** feeling genuine regret for a mistake

**Examples:**
1. The CEO issued a contrite statement following the outage.

---

### 1841. space → premises

**Meaning:** the building and land occupied by a business

**Examples:**
1. The company is relocating to larger premises downtown.
2. Only authorized staff may access the secured premises.

---

### 1842. spare → surplus

**Meaning:** more than what is needed, extra

**Examples:**
1. We redirected surplus inventory to the outlet stores.
2. The budget review revealed a small surplus this quarter.

---

### 1843. spark → ignite

**Meaning:** to cause something to start suddenly, especially interest or activity

**Examples:**
1. The keynote speech ignited a wave of new partnership inquiries.
2. The rebrand ignited renewed enthusiasm among longtime customers.

---

### 1844. speak → articulate

**Meaning:** to express an idea clearly and effectively

**Examples:**

2. The candidate struggled to articulate a concrete growth strategy.

---

### 1845. special → exceptional

**Meaning:** unusually good or outstanding

**Examples:**

2. Her exceptional grasp of the data won over the skeptical investors.

---

### 1846. speculate → conjecture

**Meaning:** to form an opinion without firm evidence

**Examples:**
1. Analysts can only conjecture about the timing of the next rate cut.
2. Employees conjectured about the reasons behind the sudden leadership change.

---

### 1847. spell out → elucidate

**Meaning:** to explain something clearly and in detail

**Examples:**

2. Please elucidate how the new policy affects remote employees.

---

### 1848. spin → characterize

**Meaning:** to describe or portray something in a particular way

**Examples:**
1. The press release characterized the layoffs as a strategic realignment.
2. Management characterized the delay as a temporary setback.

---

### 1849. split → partition

**Meaning:** to divide something into separate parts

**Examples:**

### 1883. threadbare → flimsy

### 1884. thrifty → frugal

2. Leadership aims to eradicate unnecessary approval layers by year-end.

---

### 1980. wise → prudent

**Meaning:** showing careful and sensible judgment

**Examples:**
1. It was prudent to delay the launch until testing was complete.
2. A prudent reserve fund helped the company survive the downturn.

---

### 1981. withhold → sequester

**Meaning:** to isolate or set aside, often to keep separate

**Examples:**
1. Client funds are sequestered in a separate escrow account.
2. The bonus pool was sequestered pending final audit approval.

---

### 1982. wobble → falter

**Meaning:** to lose strength or momentum, showing signs of failure

**Examples:**
1. Confidence in the platform faltered after the second outage.
2. The negotiation faltered once pricing became the sticking point.

---

### 1983. wonder → ponder

**Meaning:** to think carefully or deeply about something

**Examples:**
1. The committee pondered several options before choosing a vendor.
2. Leadership is pondering whether to expand into the European market.

---

### 1984. wordy → verbose

**Meaning:** using more words than necessary

**Examples:**
1. The client asked us to trim the verbose sections of the proposal.
2. Feedback noted the report was too verbose for a busy executive audience.

---

### 1985. worn out → depleted

**Meaning:** used up or exhausted

**Examples:**
1. The marketing budget was depleted well before the campaign ended.
2. Staff felt depleted after back-to-back product launches.

---

### 1986. wrap up → finalize

**Meaning:** to complete or settle something conclusively

**Examples:**
1. Legal expects to finalize the contract terms by Friday.
2. The team finalized the budget ahead of the board meeting.

---

### 1987. wreck → derail

**Meaning:** to cause something to fail or go off track

**Examples:**
1. A last-minute pricing dispute nearly derailed the acquisition.
2. Poor communication derailed the rollout in the first week.

---

### 1988. wrestle → grapple

**Meaning:** to struggle to deal with a difficult problem

**Examples:**
1. The team is grappling with how to cut costs without losing talent.
2. Leadership has been grappling with the decision to outsource support.

---

### 1989. xerox → replicate

**Meaning:** to reproduce something exactly

**Examples:**
1. The regional office replicated the successful onboarding process.
2. We plan to replicate this pilot's results in two more markets.

---

### 1990. x-factor → differentiator

**Meaning:** the distinctive quality that sets something apart from competitors

**Examples:**
1. Fast, personalized support is our key differentiator in this market.
2. Investors asked what the real differentiator was against larger rivals.

---

### 1991. x out → eliminate

**Meaning:** to remove or cross off completely

**Examples:**
1. We eliminated three vendors that failed the security review.
2. The shortlist was narrowed after eliminating options over budget.

---

### 1992. yell → bellow

**Meaning:** to shout loudly, often in anger or urgency

**Examples:**
1. The floor manager bellowed instructions during the system outage.
2. He bellowed across the trading floor when the numbers came in.

---

### 1993. young → nascent

**Meaning:** just beginning to develop or exist

**Examples:**
1. The nascent division has already attracted its first major client.
2. Investors are cautious about entering such a nascent market.

---

### 1994. yearn → aspire

**Meaning:** to have a strong desire to achieve something

**Examples:**
1. The startup aspires to become the market leader within five years.
2. Many employees aspire to move into leadership roles internally.

---

### 1995. yes-man → sycophant

**Meaning:** someone who flatters others to gain favor rather than offering honest input

**Examples:**
1. The board removed a known sycophant from the advisory committee.
2. Strong leaders avoid surrounding themselves with sycophants.

---

### 1996. zany → eccentric

**Meaning:** unconventional and unusual in a distinctive way

**Examples:**
1. The founder's eccentric management style became part of the company's brand.
2. Clients found the eccentric marketing campaign refreshing.

---

### 1997. zealous → fervent

**Meaning:** having or showing great enthusiasm for a cause

**Examples:**
1. A fervent believer in the mission, she rallied the team through tough quarters.
2. The sales team showed fervent support for the new commission structure.

---

### 1998. zip through → expedite

**Meaning:** to make a process happen more quickly

**Examples:**
1. We expedited the approval to meet the client's tight deadline.
2. The new software expedites invoice processing by several days.

---

### 1999. zone → domain

**Meaning:** an area of activity, interest, or responsibility

**Examples:**
1. Data governance now falls within the CTO's domain.
2. Customer retention is squarely within the marketing team's domain.

---

### 2000. zoom in on → scrutinize

**Meaning:** to examine something very closely and critically

**Examples:**
1. Auditors scrutinized every line item in the expense report.
2. The board scrutinized the acquisition terms before granting approval.
